Supervisory Intervention

14 Supervisory Intervention

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Physical AI stack focus for this chapter.

Why can handing a moving robot to a human operator cause the collision the handover was meant to prevent?

Most deployed robots keep a person in reserve for the situations their learned policies cannot handle, and that reserve is usually treated as the final safeguard. A person, however, needs a large fraction of a second to respond even while watching closely and several seconds after only monitoring. A machine moving at walking pace covers meters in that time, so a request for help can consume the very clearance the help was meant to protect. Taking over also creates hazards of its own. Two controllers that each believe they hold a joint can command it at once, a controller that believes the other still holds it can leave it unattended, and an abrupt switch between their commands jolts the drivetrain and the load it carries. A takeover that grants unchecked control lets a startled or lagging operator drive the machine into a rack. A log that does not mark who commanded each instant can neither settle what caused an incident nor keep the machine’s own near-failures out of the demonstrations it later learns from. Authority can change hands within a single control cycle, but the momentum already delivered across the causal boundary cannot be recalled, so a takeover must be engineered as a control action with its own deadline, its own limits, and its own record.

Learning Objectives
  • Specify operator permissions through capability matrices that bind each authenticated role’s operations to physical channels, preemption tiers, and clearance-derived deadlines
  • Select a state-dependent local fallback that remains physically feasible while human intervention is pending
  • Calculate the latest fallback time and takeover speed ceilings from human reaction latency and remaining physical stopping clearance
  • Evaluate the inherited chunk lease and the chosen manual lease against their modes’ stopping reserves and available fallback mechanisms
  • Design authority handshakes that keep one owner on each actuator output and size fixed-endpoint \(\Glyph{C}^2\) command blends within each channel’s rate limit
  • Diagnose mode confusion in split-channel handovers and specify the per-channel annunciation that keeps an operator’s model of ownership current
  • Evaluate whether an authority record provides the cryptographic and kinematic evidence needed to reconstruct post-incident fault causality
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

14.1 Shared Authority

When a pallet blocks an aisle and the warehouse mobile manipulator requests a remote assistant to take over, the transfer introduces a delay that no test on the chassis can bound, because it belongs to a person. Part III left the running machine with a stopping budget whose every term belongs to the hardware, each with a declared bound and a loaded test that could refute it, even where the test has not yet run, as for the dispatch-to-admission interval \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) that The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval allocated but did not measure. Part IV begins where a person enters that loop.

Supervisory intervention transfers the right to propose actions from an autonomous policy to a human operator, and eventually returns that authority to the machine. Nearly every embodied machine learning system operating in the physical world relies on this exchange. Remote teleoperation assistants clear blocked aisles and resolve scenes that warehouse robots and autonomous vehicles cannot interpret on their own. Safety drivers and teach-pendant operators step in whenever an operational policy drifts outside its design envelope, and their corrections protect the hardware and supply demonstration data for later retraining. Engineering block diagrams often reduce this supervision to a single switch or an external arrow pointing into the plant. In the physical world, however, kinetic energy does not pause while a multiplexer toggles inputs. Authority transfer is a control maneuver with its own deadlines, and it must be executed in coordination with the slowest and least predictable element in the loop, the human operator.

How often that exchange happens on a mature fleet is a matter of public record in one jurisdiction. California requires every company testing autonomous vehicles on its public roads to file annual reports of autonomous miles and of the disengagements, transfers of control to a person, that occurred during them (California Department of Motor Vehicles 2026). Figure 1 plots the filings of one long-running fleet, Waymo’s.

Over the decade the safety-driver rate rose from about 1,200 to about 19,200 miles per disengagement, yet the fleet still reported 174 takeovers in its latest year, and the series is far from monotone. Between 2020 and 2021 the rate fell 3.8× while the miles driven grew 3.7×. Driverless testing reports one disengagement every 71,800 miles, 54 in its latest year. Two consequences follow for the machine. Human authority is not a phase a capable system grows out of; even the most experienced fleet exercises it every year, so the transfer mechanism is a permanent part of the design. The reported rate also depends on where the fleet drove and on what its operator chose to count, which is why an intervention count tells a reviewer how often the exchange happened but not, by itself, how well either party performed it.

↪ The two permit series as two stages of permission to operate, and how a machine leaves one stage for the next, are developed in Permission in Stages.

The central engineering problem of supervisory intervention is how authority can change hands on a moving machine without exhausting stopping clearance, letting two controllers drive the same actuator at once, or losing track of who commanded each instant. The fourth law, permission must be earned (principle 4), frames the answer, and here Part IV applies it for the first time to a person. Like an autonomous policy, a human operator receives control authority only while valid evidence supports that grant, and loses it the moment that evidence lapses. Until the platform’s permission path checks it, every command from an operator is an untrusted request that cannot cross the causal boundary. A safe transfer must therefore bind each authenticated role to explicit actuator channels and timing bounds, commit ownership atomically on a control tick, blend reference changes within drivetrain rate limits, and log who held authority at every cycle for incident review and policy retraining.

For the warehouse mobile manipulator, this timing constraint is direct and unforgiving. When the blocked aisle prompts the machine to request a remote assistant, at its chosen 1.3 m/s aisle speed a 4.5 s pause before braking, the design out-of-loop takeover time that section 14.2.2 derives from driver take-over studies, carries the machine 5.8 m, more than five times its 1.10 m rack-end clear distance. An operator’s intervention request reaches a chassis that is already in motion under an admitted command; human intent cannot erase physical momentum or make an actuator respond in zero time. The permission path resolves this transition in two stages. The arbiter that selects among the fallback ladder’s rungs also decides, for each channel, which source holds the right to propose: the learned policy, the operator, or, once that authority lapses, a fallback rung. The enforcer then admits or rejects each setpoint from the selected source against physical envelope invariants. Both stages run deterministically on a dedicated safety microcontroller (MCU). Source selection precedes the enforcer’s check, and rung selection follows it whenever the check fails, so human intervention adds a new input to the arbiter, a person, rather than a new component. Throughout this process, the plant must keep enough clearance to bring itself to a safe halt. Switching setpoints in a single step demands a sudden torque jump that strains motor drives, gearboxes, and payload stability. Delaying the transfer, in turn, uses up the remaining stopping margin even when both the learned policy and the human supervisor produce sensible trajectories on their own.

↪ Whether a human role that needs this much time can count as a safety measure at all, and who is blamed when it cannot, is developed in Oversight a Person Can Perform.

Parasuraman, Raja, and Dietrich H. Manzey. 2010. “Complacency and Bias in Human Use of Automation: An Attentional Integration.” Human Factors 52 (3): 381–410. https://doi.org/10.1177/0018720810376055.

1 Situation Awareness Levels: Endsley (1995) set out situation awareness as three levels: perceiving the elements of a scene, comprehending what they mean together, and projecting their state forward. The levels fail separately. A remote assistant can see the pallet without projecting that the base will not stop short of it, which is why a console that reports where the operator is looking cannot shorten the response budget (section 14.6).

Endsley, Mica R. 1995. “Toward a Theory of Situation Awareness in Dynamic Systems.” Human Factors 37 (1): 32–64. https://doi.org/10.1518/001872095779049543.
Endsley, Mica R., and Esin O. Kiris. 1995. “The Out-of-the-Loop Performance Problem and Level of Control in Automation.” Human Factors 37 (2): 381–94. https://doi.org/10.1518/001872095779064555.

Because momentum and kinetic energy sit on the physical side of the causal boundary, the machine keeps moving under the last admitted setpoint while the remote operator pieces together what is happening in the aisle. That mental reconstruction is slow. It is the paradox Lisanne Bainbridge called the ironies of automation (Bainbridge 1983): engineers automate routine work to remove human error, yet leave human operators to handle the hardest, unmodeled edge cases that the automation cannot solve, where plant dynamics are abnormal and time is shortest. An operator who only monitors a screen also loses both manual feel and a current mental model of how the plant is moving. When an unexpected hazard forces a sudden takeover, that operator must act under tight time pressure with little context. Human vigilance fades quickly when watching for rare faults, and automation complacency makes the problem worse whenever secondary tasks compete for the supervisor’s attention (Parasuraman and Manzey 2010). Decades of human factors research confirm that passive monitoring erodes situation awareness1 and adds long decision delays when automation fails without warning (Endsley and Kiris 1995). The machine must therefore protect its own collision-free stopping envelope throughout the takeover window. Faster networks can trim milliseconds from packet transport, but they cannot shorten the time a person needs to see a hazard, decide what to do, and move a hand control.

↩︎ The causal boundary, across which admitted motion cannot be recalled, is established in Defining the Causal Boundary.

Cutting power to a moving base lets it coast, whereas cutting power to a loaded manipulator arm without a holding brake drops the payload. Each stage of an intervention therefore needs a fallback matched to the machine’s current state: a moving base may need controlled regenerative braking followed by a mechanical brake once stopped, while an arm carrying a tote needs an active, gravity-compensated position hold. Each response depends on the specific sensors and power rails qualified to carry it out. Because local damping, torque inhibition, and full power isolation produce different physical trajectories, the system cannot treat them as interchangeable steps on a generic escalation ladder; a protective input must invoke the validated fallback built for the plant’s current motion state.

↩︎ The fallback ladder, from which the arbiter selects a state-matched protective response on every tick, is established in The Fallback Ladder.

Autonomous and manual proposal rights operate under separate, explicit leases. The learned policy proposes trajectories under the action-chunk lease established in Chunk Lease Sizing from the Stopping Budget, while a teleoperation session receives a distinct, clearance-budgeted manual lease. Rejecting an invalid replacement command from an operator does not invalidate an active, previously admitted setpoint whose evidence, state bounds, and lease duration remain healthy. If either controller exhausts its lease duration or violates its underlying safety premises, the permission path immediately triggers the appropriate local fallback.

The permission path prevents split-brain command contention by pairing an upstream source arbiter with exclusive actuator-output ownership. In the 1 kHz control architecture analyzed here, the right to propose on a set of channels is a single object, the proposal token, and the arbiter grants it to one source at a time. An authority commit revokes the learned policy’s proposal token and latches its last approved setpoint; the admission enforcer remains the sole, non-bypassable writer to the physical actuators before, during, and after the transition. Standard bus protocols such as Controller Area Network (CAN) arbitration serialize message frames from multiple transmitters, but bus-level arbitration does not establish control-layer authority. If two nodes broadcast conflicting yet syntactically valid frames to the same motor drive, the actuator chatters between setpoints and loses stability. Gating the output path in firmware and selecting a single source per tick prevents that contention. Across continuous state spaces, the enforcer executes a bumpless transfer, a command handoff without reference step discontinuities (section 14.4), interpolating between validated endpoints while enforcing command-rate limits and honoring measured plant limits.

Intervention events also directly shape the machine learning lifecycle. If a flight recorder or logging pipeline captures a rescued episode without marking the exact handover instant, imitation algorithms treat the near-failure states that triggered the rescue as if they were intentional expert demonstrations. As Intervention and Recovery Data establishes, the states visited by the learner right before a takeover supply training data for recovery policies, but autonomous commands from a failing interval require review rather than automatic admission as expert targets. Downstream learning algorithms, from Dataset Aggregation (DAgger) (Ross et al. 2011) to modern human-gated policy correction methods (section 14.8.3), depend on knowing which controller held authority over each logged timestep. To resolve this attribution, the architecture keeps an authority record (the twelfth record in the platform’s record chain, specified in section 14.8), which retains the captured evidence preceding and following the takeover, with explicit gaps and timing bounds. Its per-tick entries timestamp every transition against the safety microcontroller’s monotonic clock. An authority commit ends policy ownership, ensuring that autonomous exploration, the enforcer’s transition blend, and verified human recovery remain distinct, auditable intervals.

Every shared-authority design begins by defining which named intervention may act upon which physical channel and establishing the maximum latency the machine can tolerate while awaiting human action. The protocol handshakes, setpoint blending algorithms, and authority records that follow all rest upon this initial grant of authority.

14.2 Human Authority, Reaction Time, and Clearance

An assistant guiding the arm can move the chassis if its credential also permits base-drive commands. A command admitted under that permission performs mechanical work on the plant before software can revoke it. Authority must therefore identify the physical channels a person may affect, rather than grant unrestricted access through a generic operator role.

The remote assistant who clears a blocked aisle and the coworker who takes an item from the gripper act on the same machine, and neither should hold the other’s authority. Each grant therefore names an operation together with its timing budget, physical degrees of freedom, and preemption rule. The permission path keeps these grants in an explicit capability matrix, a permissions table mapping individual operations (such as an emergency power cutoff, a velocity trim adjustment, a trajectory path veto, or manual drive of the base and arm) to authenticated roles. The design reflects classic taxonomies of human supervisory control2 (Sheridan 1992) and multi-level automation (Parasuraman et al. 2000). Each entry in this matrix defines the exact actuator channels the operation governs, the preemption tier it carries over the learned policy, and the maximum propagation latency permitted from physical input to actuator response.

2 Supervisory Control Levels: Sheridan and Verplank (1978) formulated a ten-level taxonomy of supervisory control, running from a human who performs the whole task without computer assistance to a computer that decides and acts while ignoring the human.

Sheridan, Thomas B., and William L. Verplank. 1978. Human and Computer Control of Undersea Teleoperators. Technical Report N00014-77-C-0256. Man-Machine Systems Laboratory, Massachusetts Institute of Technology.
Sheridan, Thomas B. 2021. “Human Supervisory Control of Automation.” In Handbook of Human Factors and Ergonomics, 5th ed., edited by Gavriel Salvendy and Waldemar Karwowski. Wiley. https://doi.org/10.1002/9781119636113.ch28.

Sheridan also describes the supervisor’s work as five steps in sequence: plan the task off-line, teach the plan to the computer, monitor the automatic action, intervene when it fails or the goal changes, and learn from the episode (Sheridan 2021). Intervening in flight and learning from the episode require finer granularity than a single autonomy level assigned to the whole task. The mobile manipulator needs an authority assignment per channel and per instant: during a takeover, the arm can sit near the manual end of the scale while the base still runs at the automatic end, and the enforcer holds the final admission gate on every channel throughout because it checks the operator’s commands just as it checks the policy’s. Levels that differ across channels are what a supervisor can lose track of, the mode confusion of section 14.6, so a channel left out of a transfer keeps its autonomous owner only while the console announces that it does, and otherwise holds. Each entry in the capability matrix therefore names the exact operation and channel it governs.

Naming the operations separates intervention from safety enforcement, two mechanisms that system architectures routinely conflate. Enforcement withholds permission from a candidate action proposed by a learned model, modifying, clamping, or projecting the setpoint while leaving the machine under autonomous control. Intervention transfers authority away from the model to an external entity, terminating autonomous operation and giving the supervisor the right to propose, while every command still passes the permission path. An enforcement filter sits in the fast inner control loop (1 kHz / \(\le\) 1 ms) to prevent the policy from demanding an over-current maneuver or violating a barrier certificate (Ames et al. 2019) during nominal tracking. An intervention mechanism strips the policy of its proposal token when an operational boundary is breached or an operator demands control. A system equipped with enforcement but lacking intervention cannot be redirected when human intent changes or external conditions fall outside the policy design envelope, leading to conservative deadlock when barrier constraints collapse the feasible control set. Conversely, a system equipped with intervention but lacking enforcement permits a lagging operator to drive the plant into structural failure during nominal manual operation. Table 1 sets the two mechanisms side by side, from trigger and authority holder to typical failure.

Ames, Aaron D., Samuel Coogan, Magnus Egerstedt, Gennaro Notomista, Koushil Sreenath, and Paulo Tabuada. 2019. “Control Barrier Functions: Theory and Applications.” 2019 18th European Control Conference (ECC), 3420–31. https://doi.org/10.23919/ECC.2019.8796030.

↩︎ The actuator current and thermal limits an enforcement filter must respect are established in The Five Physical Budgets.

Definition 14.1: Supervisory intervention

Supervisory intervention is the transfer of the right to propose commands on named actuator channels between a learned policy and an authenticated person, committed on a control tick and bounded by a lease, while every command continues to pass the same permission check.

  1. Significance: The transfer is a delay on a moving machine, so it is priced in the stopping budget \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) rather than in the user interface, and the waiting before braking, not the braking itself, decides whether the machine stops in time.
  2. Distinction: Enforcement changes a proposal and leaves the proposer in place; intervention changes the proposer and leaves the check in place. A machine needs both, because neither can do the other’s job.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that a takeover hands the operator the actuators. The operator gains only the right to propose, and the machine still needs a local stop that does not wait for the operator to act.

14.2.1 Capability matrices and the intervention-enforcement split

On the warehouse mobile manipulator, a coworker taking a mug from the gripper at the packing station would be at risk if their station credential also allowed them to command the arm’s joints or drive the base. The capability matrix prevents that cross-channel hazard by letting the remote assistant redirect motion while denying any motion authority to the coworker accepting an item.

In that matrix, the remote assistant who supervises the mobile manipulator may hold a signed trajectory veto capability for the base’s planner channel: role remote_assistant, operation veto_path, channel planner_proposal, preemption tier above the learned planner but below the local enforcer, authenticated session and nonce, and a clearance-derived acknowledgment deadline. The enforcer responds to a valid veto with a checked local hold or stop. The same session carries a second entry, manual_drive, on the base-velocity and arm-joint channels, which becomes active only through a handover that commits within the clearance-derived deadline, and lapses with the manual lease of section 14.3. Every command it carries still passes the permission check. A wheel drive-torque packet from that credential is denied, because neither entry grants the base’s wheel drive-torque channel.

The coworker at the packing station holds a different entry, accept_item, which lets the coworker take from the gripper the mug the arm picked from the takeaway conveyor. That entry grants no motion authority. The item-handover channel of the enforcement record caps the arm’s approach to the coworker’s hand at 0.10 m/s, and no capability lifts that cap. The handover is a collaborative application in the sense of ISO 10218-2:2025 (International Organization for Standardization 2025b), and the body model of the hand that bounds it, with the contact force that model predicts, is set out once in Intentional contact and in-contact energy barriers. ISO 10218-2 leaves out the hazards of mounting the arm on a mobile platform, which ANSI/A3 R15.08-2 addresses for the running machine (Association for Advancing Automation 2023).

International Organization for Standardization. 2025b. ISO 10218-2:2025: Robotics, Safety Requirements, Part 2: Industrial Robot Applications and Robot Cells. International Organization for Standardization.
Association for Advancing Automation. 2023. ANSI/A3 R15.08-2-2023: Industrial Mobile Robots, Safety Requirements, Part 2: Requirements for IMR System(s) and IMR Application(s). Association for Advancing Automation.

The ceiling behind that cap comes from the contact-force model (equation 12.13). For a free hand, which recoils, the selected 50 N criterion admits up to 0.24 m/s, and the cap sits under that ceiling. A hand clamped against a rack upright or the arm itself cannot recoil, and the same criterion then admits only 0.053 m/s, below the cap. The cap therefore holds only if the handover geometry excludes clamping, a premise the release case must evidence. The ceiling is conditional on these inputs and does not by itself establish a pressure or injury limit for a particular contact geometry; the site’s risk assessment qualifies the effective mass, the force criterion, and the contact geometry before the cap is qualified.

↪ The release criterion that holds the handover’s contact force under its assessed limit is developed in Physical Criteria for Release.

Each capability entry binds actor, operation, channel, deadline, authentication, preemption tier, and fallback; a valid signature alone grants no motion authority. Physical clearance sets the tolerable deadline; the operator’s response time determines whether a human can meet it.

14.2.2 Human reaction time and travel before braking

The remote assistant must notice the blocked aisle, decide how to recover, move the controls, and deliver a checked request. Summed in sequence, the total supervisory takeover latency \(\SupervisoryTakeoverLatency\) is: \[\SupervisoryTakeoverLatency = \OperatorSensoryTime + \OperatorCognitiveTime + \OperatorMotorTime + \ConsoleCommunicationTime \tag{1}\] The first three terms belong to the person and depend on engagement and expectation; the last belongs to the console and the link. Human response times are distributions with long right tails, so a stopping budget needs a value that nearly every request meets, not the typical one. The calculation below compares a continuously engaged operator with an out-of-loop assistant. Its communication term includes transport and the protocol exchange, so that exchange must not be charged again.

Waiting consumes distance before the selected stop begins. The finished stopping budget from Sizing the composite stopping budget supplies the machine delay, resident stop, and fixed allowances. Adding the human waiting interval exposes whether remote rescue can fit at the current speed; it does not authorize waiting when the local stop is already required.

The budgets that follow are illustrative design comparisons, not measured takeover performance. Each of them must also pay for the dispatch-to-admission interval \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\), which The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval allocated but has not yet measured. A takeover does not remove that interval. The policy must still infer on the frame that shows the blocked aisle before it can request help, and its request, like any proposal, reaches the arbiter only through the safety microcontroller, so the base keeps rolling through \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) before the human response even begins. This chapter calls a budget without the interval the partial budget and a budget with it the complete budget. The stops and overruns below use the complete budget. The speed ceilings and deadlines give the partial value first and then the charged one, because the gap between the two is itself a design lesson.

The stopping budget of The warehouse mobile manipulator's stopping budget shows what equation 1 costs on the mobile manipulator. At the 1.3 m/s aisle speed it leaves 131.0 mm of the 1.10 m rack-end clear distance before dispatch-to-admission travel, and 48.7 mm once the 82.3 mm of travel during \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) is charged. A takeover delay adds to the machine’s own pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\), and every millisecond of it becomes travel at the aisle speed:

  1. Mode 1: Continuous in-loop teleoperation: An attending operator with hands on the controls of a synchronized leader arm or a real-time console, responding to the stimulus the task has led them to expect, makes a simple reaction. The Model Human Processor3 (Card et al. 1983) prices that reaction as one perceptual, one cognitive, and one motor cycle (Jastrzembski and Charness 2007). At the slow end of each cycle’s range, \(\OperatorSensoryTime =\) 200 ms, \(\OperatorCognitiveTime =\) 170 ms, and \(\OperatorMotorTime =\) 100 ms. The console adds \(\ConsoleCommunicationTime =\) 135 ms: the scene reaches the operator after a video glass-to-glass latency4 of 104 ms, measured for a wired teleoperation link (Schimpe et al. 2022), and the request returns through a 31 ms protocol exchange, the four-phase handshake that section 14.3.3 builds and budgets phase by phase. By equation 1, \(\TakeoverResponseTime =\) 605 ms. The machine travels 786.5 mm during that delay, several times the spare margin. Solving the complete budget for speed, with \(\TakeoverResponseTime\) and \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) added to \(\PrebrakeDelay\), bounds the machine to 0.797 m/s. The value holds only for the expected stimulus. A driver who is waiting for a brake signal needs up to 0.75 s to detect it and reach the pedal, and a surprise raises that to about 1.5 s (Green 2000). Section 14.2.3 prices those cases.
  2. Mode 2: Passive out-of-the-loop supervisory takeover: An assistant who monitors the telemetry of several machines passively does not make a simple reaction, and its stages cannot be priced separately. Driver take-over studies measure the whole interval, from the take-over request to the first control input. Across 520 experimental conditions in 129 studies, the mean take-over time averaged 2.72 s, and the distribution of those condition means is skewed to the right (Zhang et al. 2019). For this illustrative comparison, the selected design value is the 90th percentile of the condition means, 4.33 s, computed from the study’s published data. With the same 135 ms console term, the takeover delay is \(\DisengagedTakeoverResponseTime =\) 4.5 s, during which the machine covers 5.80 m, more than five times the clear distance, before any brake command. The same solution bounds the machine to 0.189 m/s.

3 Model Human Processor: Card et al. (1983) modeled a person responding to a stimulus as three processors in series, perceptual, cognitive, and motor, and gave each cycle time a typical value and a range rather than a single number. The range is the design input. A budget built on the typical cycles admits a speed that a slower but still normal operator overruns, so this chapter’s design row takes the slow end of every range.

Card, Stuart K., Thomas P. Moran, and Allen Newell. 1983. The Psychology of Human-Computer Interaction. Lawrence Erlbaum Associates.

4 Glass-to-Glass Latency: The interval from light entering the remote camera’s lens to the matching image leaving the operator’s display, covering exposure, encoding, transport, decoding, and display refresh. It is the only video delay the operator experiences, and every stage of that path contributes to it, so a value measured on a wired link does not carry over to a warehouse wireless link. The site must measure its own link before the value enters the takeover budget.

In Mode 2 at the aisle speed, the machine would reach a static obstacle at the rack end at full speed, because contact occurs before braking ever begins. Stronger braking can raise a finite stopping-speed ceiling, but cannot recover distance already spent waiting. As braking distance tends toward zero, reaction travel still sets a limiting ceiling. A percentile of condition means also understates the tail an individual operator can produce, because each condition’s own spread grows with its mean; the site must measure its operators, console, and task load before any of these values admits a speed. Road regulation sizes the same interval generously for a vehicle that can keep driving while it waits. For an unanswered transition demand in the cited 2021 version of UN Regulation No. 157, an automated lane-keeping system begins its minimum risk manoeuvre no earlier than 10 s after it issues the demand (United Nations Economic Commission for Europe 2021), 2.2× the design takeover here. Severe system or vehicle failures permit an immediate minimum risk manoeuvre, and an obstacle can require an emergency response during the transition phase. The vehicle can afford that wait because it stays in its lane under automated control throughout. At the aisle speed the same wait would carry the mobile manipulator 13 m, so the machine cannot borrow the road’s interval, only its structure of a demand, a bounded wait, and an automatic fallback. Section 14.2.4 turns the two design values into the speeds at which the machine may request each kind of takeover.

Drawn to scale, the Mode 1 budget shows where the clearance goes (figure 2). The 48.7 mm spare of the complete budget corresponds to 37.5 ms at the 1.3 m/s aisle speed, so the machine has spent it long before the 104 ms video stage ends and before the operator has perceived anything. The perceptual, cognitive, and motor cycles and the handshake that follow are all travel the budget cannot absorb, 786.5 mm in total.

Napkin Math 14.1: The takeover tax
Problem: At the 1.3 m/s aisle speed, does the finished stopping budget still fit the rack-end clear distance once an in-loop or an out-of-loop takeover delay is inserted before braking, and by how much does each overrun it?

Variables:

  • \(\SpeedAtDetection =\) 1.3 m/s (Chosen) and \(\ClearDistance =\) 1.10 m (Illustrative).
  • \(\PrebrakeDelay =\) 133.6 ms, the machine’s own pre-brake delay (Derived; The warehouse mobile manipulator's stopping budget), which excludes the dispatch-to-admission interval, and \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval =\) 63.3 ms, the allocation of The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval (Illustrative), which a takeover still spends before braking.
  • \(\ResidentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection) = \SpeedAtDetection^2/(2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration) + \SpeedAtDetection\,\CredibleBrakingDeceleration/(2\JerkLimit)\), the jerk-limited resident stop of equation 11.17, with \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration =\) 2 m/s² (Illustrative) and \(\JerkLimit =\) 7.11 m/s³ (Derived).
  • \(\FixedClearanceAllowance = \LocalizationBound + \ProtectiveClearanceMargin + \TrackingErrorBound =\) 190 mm (Illustrative and Chosen).
  • \(\TakeoverResponseTime =\) 605 ms in-loop and \(\DisengagedTakeoverResponseTime =\) 4.5 s out-of-loop (Derived, from the measured human and transport terms of Modes 1 and 2 plus the chosen handshake).

Math: Every term is in millimeters. The machine alone, charged with the dispatch-to-admission travel, leaves a 48.7 mm spare:

\[d_{\text{stop},0} = v\,\tau_{\text{delay}} + v\,\DispatchAdmissionInterval + d_{\text{suffix}}(v) + \delta = 173.68 + 82.30 + 605.31 + 190 = 1051.30\text{ mm}\]

Each takeover adds \(\SpeedAtDetection\,\SupervisoryTakeoverLatency\) before the same stop:

\[d_{\text{stop},1} = v\,\tau_{\text{delay}} + v\,\DispatchAdmissionInterval + v\,T_{\Sigma,1} + d_{\text{suffix}}(v) + \delta = 173.68 + 82.30 + 786.5 + 605.31 + 190 = 1837.80\text{ mm}\]

\[d_{\text{stop},2} = v\,\tau_{\text{delay}} + v\,\DispatchAdmissionInterval + v\,T_{\Sigma,2} + d_{\text{suffix}}(v) + \delta = 173.68 + 82.30 + 5804.5 + 605.31 + 190 = 6855.80\text{ mm}\]

Result: The in-loop takeover overruns the clear distance by 737.8 mm and the out-of-loop takeover by 5.76 m; the second stop is 3.7× the first.

Systems insight: The waiting term, not the braking term, decides the verdict. The in-loop takeover alone covers 16.1× the spare, so no stronger brake can make \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) hold at this speed; only a lower \(\SpeedAtDetection\) or a stop before the request can. The delay, braking, and allowance terms follow the stopping-distance model of Kinematic Stopping Envelopes and Information Age Lag.

14.2.3 Takeover speed ceilings from available clearance

Because no braking force can recover distance spent waiting, the machine must bound its operating speed before an intervention is ever requested. One inequality sets both bounds that this section and the next derive. If the machine continues at speed \(\SpeedAtDetection\) for a waiting duration \(\ExternalWait\) while awaiting the operator’s response, the finished stopping budget of Sizing the composite stopping budget gains one term, the travel during that wait. The pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\), the jerk-limited resident stop \(\ResidentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection)\) (equation 11.17), and the fixed allowances keep the meanings defined there, and the sum must still not exceed \(\ClearDistance\) measured at the same reference event: \[\StoppingDistance(\ExternalWait) = \SpeedAtDetection(\ExternalWait + \PrebrakeDelay) + \ResidentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection) + \LocalizationBound + \ProtectiveClearanceMargin + \TrackingErrorBound \le \ClearDistance. \tag{2}\] Setting \(\ExternalWait = \SupervisoryTakeoverLatency\) and solving for speed gives the takeover speed ceiling of this section. Fixing the speed and solving for \(\ExternalWait\) gives the latest fallback deadline of section 14.2.4.

The three stops of notebook 14.1 differ only in how long the machine coasts before an identical jerk-limited resident stop begins (figure 3). Every trace ends with the same braking curve, so the whole difference between stopping 48.7 mm short of the clear distance and stopping at 6.86 m is coasting.

Table 2 prices each sourced response time. Every row adds the 135 ms console term to the human response, adds the total to \(\PrebrakeDelay\), and solves the finished stopping budget for the largest speed at which the machine still stops inside the rack-end clear distance. Before charging the missing admission delay, the ceiling falls from 1.01 m/s for an attending operator who meets the expected stimulus at the nominal pace to 0.12 m/s for the slowest percentile of takeover conditions. No row fits at the 1.3 m/s aisle speed. Two facts dominate the table. Expectation matters more than the console, because moving from an expected to a surprising stimulus costs more speed than the whole console term does. The tail matters more than the average, because a speed chosen for the mean take-over time is overrun by every condition slower than the mean.

A ceiling above the resident stop’s transition speed \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration^2/\JerkLimit\) is the positive root derived for the machine’s own stop in equation 12.2, where the half jerk ramp acts as an added delay before full braking (the rigid-stop form is equation 2.3). A takeover adds one more such interval, so setting \(\ExternalWait = \SupervisoryTakeoverLatency\) in equation 2 folds the human delay into the same root: \[\SpeedBound(\SupervisoryTakeoverLatency) = \CredibleBrakingDeceleration\left[\sqrt{\FullSpeedTravelTime^2 + \frac{2\,(\ClearDistance-\FixedClearanceAllowance)}{\CredibleBrakingDeceleration}} - \FullSpeedTravelTime\right],\qquad \FullSpeedTravelTime = \PrebrakeDelay + \SupervisoryTakeoverLatency + \frac{\CredibleBrakingDeceleration}{2\JerkLimit}. \tag{3}\] The term \(\FullSpeedTravelTime\) collects every interval during which the machine still moves at \(\SpeedAtDetection\): its own delay, the human’s, and the half jerk ramp before full braking. Below \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration^2/\JerkLimit\) the stop never reaches full deceleration and the cubic branch of equation 11.17 applies instead, which is why the table’s slower rows come from the solver rather than from this root.

Napkin Math 14.2: The speed a human buys
Problem: What is the highest speed at which the mobile manipulator may request an in-loop takeover from an attending operator, and does any brake, however strong, admit the aisle speed?

Variables:

  • \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration =\) 2 m/s² (Illustrative) and \(\JerkLimit =\) 7.11 m/s³ (Derived).
  • \(\PrebrakeDelay =\) 0.1336 s (Derived) and \(\TakeoverResponseTime =\) 0.605 s (Derived, from the measured human and transport terms of Mode 1 plus the chosen handshake).
  • \(\ClearDistance - \FixedClearanceAllowance =\) 0.91 m (Illustrative, less the fixed allowances).

Math: \(\FullSpeedTravelTime =\) 0.1336 \(+\) 0.605 \(+\) 2 \(/ (2 \times\) 7.11\() =\) 0.1336 \(+\) 0.605 \(+\) 0.1406 \(=\) 0.8792 s. Then \(\FullSpeedTravelTime^2 + 2 \times\) 0.91 \(/\) 2 \(=\) 1.6830 s², whose square root is 1.2973 s, so by equation 3 \(\SpeedBound =\) 2 \(\times\) (1.2973 \(-\) 0.8792). The root lies above \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration^2/\JerkLimit =\) 0.562 m/s, so the quadratic branch was the right one. If both the deceleration and jerk bounds are removed so that the resident stopping distance tends to zero, the limiting ceiling is \((\ClearDistance - \FixedClearanceAllowance)/(\PrebrakeDelay + \SupervisoryTakeoverLatency)\).

Result: \(\SpeedBound =\) 0.836 m/s, the design in-loop row of table 2; instantaneous braking would raise it only to 1.23 m/s.

Systems insight: Even a perfect brake leaves the ceiling below the 1.3 m/s aisle speed, because the travel during \(\PrebrakeDelay + \SupervisoryTakeoverLatency\) alone exceeds the clear distance at that speed. The waiting term of \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) sets the request speed, so faster brakes cannot buy back a slow human; adding the 63.3 ms admission allocation to \(\FullSpeedTravelTime\) lowers the root to 0.797 m/s.

The table also locates the other interfaces a site might offer. Continuous velocity guidance or supervisory waypoint dispatch places the operator somewhere between an expected-stimulus reaction and an out-of-loop takeover, and only a measured response distribution for that interface, console, and task says where. A hardwired protective input removes the console term but not the human response, so its operator still needs the local stop to hold the machine safe while that response runs.

14.2.4 Clearance-derived fallback deadlines

If a machine reaches the edge of its operating domain at speed and requests a takeover, an operator who is distracted, asleep, or incapacitated will fail to complete the handshake before the machine runs out of clearance. An autonomous policy’s handover request can therefore never wait indefinitely, nor can its timeout be a fixed software constant: a single fixed window that is safe at a crawl will overrun the clearance at transport speed (section 14.9 prices such a window at both speeds). Safe timeouts must instead be derived at runtime from the machine’s current speed, line-of-sight clearance, and braking deceleration.

The deadline is the second reading of the stopping inequality. The speed ceiling fixed the wait and solved for speed; the deadline fixes the current speed and solves for the wait. Setting equality in equation 2 (\(\StoppingDistance(\ExternalWait) = \ClearDistance\)) and solving for the maximum permissible waiting time yields the latest fallback deadline \(\FallbackDeadline\): \[\FallbackDeadline = \frac{\ClearDistance-\LocalizationBound-\ProtectiveClearanceMargin-\TrackingErrorBound-\ResidentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection)}{\SpeedAtDetection}-\PrebrakeDelay,\qquad \HandoverTimeout=\max\!\left(0,\min(\HandoverTimeoutCeiling,\FallbackDeadline)\right). \tag{4}\] For a live check, the safety microcontroller dispatches fallback no later than the last permission tick before the resulting deadline. A nonpositive \(\FallbackDeadline\) calls for immediate fallback and may mean stopping is already infeasible. The configured protocol ceiling cannot extend that physical deadline or the expiry of active permission.

Napkin Math 14.3: The waiting allowance
Problem: How long may the mobile manipulator wait after a handover request before it must dispatch its own stop, at the 1.3 m/s aisle speed and at the 0.75 m/s slow-down, and does the 605 ms in-loop takeover fit either allowance?

Variables:

  • \(\ClearDistance - \FixedClearanceAllowance =\) 0.91 m (Illustrative) and \(\PrebrakeDelay =\) 0.1336 s (Derived).
  • \(\ResidentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection) = \SpeedAtDetection^2/(2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration) + \SpeedAtDetection\,\CredibleBrakingDeceleration/(2\JerkLimit)\) (Derived; equation 11.17): 0.6053 m at 1.3 m/s and 0.2461 m at 0.75 m/s.
  • \(\TakeoverResponseTime =\) 605 ms (Derived, measured human and transport terms plus the chosen handshake) and \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval =\) 63.3 ms (Illustrative; the unmeasured allocation of The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval).

Math: By equation 4, \(\FallbackDeadline =\) (0.91 \(-\) 0.6053)/1.3 \(-\) 0.1336 \(=\) 0.1008 s at the aisle speed, and (0.91 \(-\) 0.2461)/0.75 \(-\) 0.1336 \(=\) 0.7516 s at the slow-down. Reserving the takeover leaves 751.6 ms \(-\) 605 ms \(=\) 146.6 ms, and charging \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) leaves 83.3 ms.

Result: At the aisle speed the takeover overruns a 100.8 ms allowance; at the slow-down it fits with 83.3 ms to spare on the complete budget.

Systems insight: Slowing from 1.3 to 0.75 m/s multiplies the waiting allowance 7.5×, because both the stop term and the travel per millisecond of \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) shrink with speed. The deadline is a function of the current state, so no single configured timeout can be correct at both speeds.

At the aisle speed, the 100.8 ms of notebook 14.3, or 131.0 mm of travel, is the unallocated timing allowance of the stopping subtotal in Sizing the composite stopping budget, and it is a partial-budget value. Charging the 63.3 ms dispatch-to-admission allocation of The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval completes the budget. At speed \(\SpeedAtDetection\) the charge costs \(\SpeedAtDetection\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) of travel, so at the aisle speed only 37.5 ms of waiting survives it. Wherever the rest of the chapter reports a waiting allowance, it gives the partial value and then the charged one, and the charged value is the one a design must meet. Rounded down to whole permission ticks, the partial aisle-speed allowance of 100.8 ms becomes 100 ms, which illustrates tick rounding rather than an absolute fallback time. The 500 ms configured ceiling is only an outer protocol limit, because the clearance deadline binds first.

Both readings of the stopping inequality also need a clock origin, and the chapter uses the two that table 3 sets side by side. The design budget behind every displayed number counts from the observation’s capture. A live check on the safety microcontroller counts from now.

The two ways to mix them fail in opposite directions. A live check that takes the current clearance, which already reflects the travel since capture, and then also subtracts the capture-origin delay pays for the elapsed part of that delay twice and dispatches its fallback early, which costs availability. The reverse mix, the capture-time clearance with a delay counted only from now, never pays for the travel since capture and dispatches its fallback late, which costs clearance.

The 31 ms protocol exchange fits the aisle-speed allowance on both budgets. The sourced 605 ms in-loop response does not. It exceeds the aisle-speed allowance several times over, so an in-loop request needs a slower machine, and the human response time, not the machine, sets how much slower. Assuming a faster human gives the wrong answer. A slow-down to 1.2 m/s would suit an operator who responds in a small fraction of a second; there the budget leaves 184.1 ms, and the sourced response overruns it by 420.9 ms. Such a design passes every check that uses an optimistic human and fails the first operator who needs the time the literature measures.

The handshake fits the aisle-speed allowance; the whole takeover needs the slow-down.

The design slows to 0.75 m/s before requesting an in-loop takeover. That speed comes from solving the budget with both the sourced response and \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) added to \(\PrebrakeDelay\), which gives a complete-budget ceiling of 0.797 m/s, rounded down to the next 0.05 m/s step. At that speed the capture-origin budget leaves 563.7 mm, or 751.6 ms of additional delay. Reserving the response leaves 146.6 ms, and charging \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) still leaves 83.3 ms. That is a conservative design allocation, not a claim that every request at that speed receives the same live timeout, and it holds only while the operator meets the expected stimulus. An unexpected hazard moves the operator to the slower rows of table 2, whose ceilings the slow-down exceeds.

Solving with both intervals matters. The partial-budget ceiling, with the response alone added, is 0.84 m/s, and a designer who stopped there might choose 0.8 m/s, which sits under it. At that speed the response leaves 58.3 ms, less than the 63.3 ms allocation, so once the omitted interval is charged the slow-down misses its deadline by 5.0 ms. A budget that leaves out one interval of a few tens of milliseconds admits a speed that the complete budget rejects.

The out-of-loop takeover forces a larger change. Its 4.5 s design response caps the request speed at 0.19 m/s, so the crawl before an out-of-loop request is 0.15 m/s, where the budget leaves 5.79 s and the design response leaves 1.32 s of it unspent. The crawl survives the \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) charge, which lowers its ceiling only to 0.189 m/s. It still fails the 99th-percentile condition, whose 7.1 s response admits only 0.12 m/s, and an individual operator’s tail is longer than any condition mean. A crawl slow enough for every operator is no longer motion worth keeping. The robust design therefore stops the machine automatically before handing over. The local stop runs first, and the out-of-loop takeover begins from standstill, where waiting costs time but no clearance. Both moving candidates still require qualified admission delay, current clearance, and a feasible stopping continuation. The hidden pallet leaves no room for an anticipatory slow-down at all, so the machine answers it with its own local stop and requests the takeover from standstill. A planned slow-down or crawl can prepare an anticipated request; neither permits waiting at aisle speed for a newly discovered obstacle.

Every one of these designs rests on two properties the chapter has so far assumed. Authority, once granted, must not outlive a proposer that falls silent, and it must move between proposers on a single tick without two of them ever commanding the same channel. Section 14.3 builds both.

Checkpoint 14.1: Takeover budgets and speed ceilings

A takeover is a delay inserted before braking, so every question about it is a question about the stopping budget.

14.3 Authority Transitions

When a remote assistant takes manual control of the arm after the mobile base has slowed or stopped for a blocked aisle, authority must move between proposers on a single permission tick without two of them ever commanding the same channel, and it must not outlive a proposer that falls silent. The transfer names each actuator channel and assigns it to a single owner; linear blending of two competing proposers is no substitute for choosing who is in charge. The local enforcer remains the sole writer to the physical actuator bus, generating a checked transition profile only after an ownership change has committed on a control tick boundary.

Discrete handover is not the only way to share control. Shared autonomy systems keep both proposers active and blend their actions continuously. In standard policy-blending methods, the robot infers the operator’s intended goal and arbitrates between automated assistance and manual control; user studies show that this arbitration works only while the robot predicts the human’s goal with high confidence (Dragan and Srinivasa 2013). That confidence breaks down when there is more than one path around an obstacle. When an operator steers left around a pallet while the autonomous policy steers right, a convex combination of two sensible commands produces a third trajectory that hits the obstacle. In those cases, a qualified arbiter must select or construct one admissible proposal; the permission path here selects a single source deterministically at each tick. Continuous blending works well when the task has a single clear goal and every intermediate blend is admissible, as when an operator makes fine adjustments to an end-effector pose. Even in purely manual operation, the safety enforcer acts as an invariant filter rather than a proposer, projecting every human command onto the admissible control set regardless of who holds the channel. Before ownership can move between sources, however, each source’s hold on a channel must be bounded in time so that a proposer that falls silent loses authority instead of leaving its last command in force.

Dragan, Anca D., and Siddhartha S. Srinivasa. 2013. “A Policy-Blending Formalism for Shared Control.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 32 (7): 790–805. https://doi.org/10.1177/0278364913490324.

↩︎ The minimal-intervention principle, which keeps an admissible proposal and applies the smallest feasible correction otherwise, is established in The Minimal Intervention Principle and Optimization Filters.

14.3.1 Execution leases and three-position enabling hardware

A stalled policy and a disconnected teleoperator leave the same immediate problem, because in both cases the moving machine still holds a previously admitted command. An inference stall, lost wireless link, or incapacitated operator must not leave that command active indefinitely. Expiring the proposer’s authority must also select a local response that brakes the machine or holds the load.

The permission path bounds autonomous chunks and manual sessions with execution leases, enforced on the safety microcontroller’s clock. The autonomous lease is the running 60 ms chunk lease. The manual lease is a separate value, chosen in section 14.3.4 and checked against the remaining clearance in section 14.4.6. An authenticated renewal must also carry valid state and evidence, because a heartbeat alone cannot refresh old observations. A wireless console that carries these renewals is a cableless control system in the sense of IEC 62745, which specifies the functions and interfaces of radio and infrared links between operator control stations, including portable ones, and a machine’s control system (International Electrotechnical Commission 2017). Formally, let \(\LeaseRefreshInstant\) denote the local hardware timestamp of the most recent renewal accepted after authentication, freshness, state, and evidence validation. At physical time \(\TimeInstant\), the lease-age condition is: \[\TimeInstant - \LeaseRefreshInstant < \ChunkLease \ \text{(autonomous)}, \qquad \TimeInstant - \LeaseRefreshInstant < \ManualLease \ \text{(manual)}. \tag{5}\] The clock advances toward expiry even when packets stop arriving. At expiry, the microcontroller revokes the affected proposal rights and selects the validated response from the current physical state. Authority can return only through the declared recovery and fresh-admission procedure; packet resumption alone cannot restore it.

International Electrotechnical Commission. 2017. IEC 62745:2017: Safety of Machinery, Requirements for Cableless Control Systems of Machinery. International Electrotechnical Commission.

5 Manual Reduced Speed: ISO 10218-1:2025 limits the tool speed of an industrial robot in manual reduced-speed mode to \(250\text{ mm/s}\) (International Organization for Standardization 2025a), the mode in which the pendant of figure 4 couples motion to a three-position grip. Releasing or over-squeezing the grip only requests a stop from the safety circuit, and the performance level that circuit must reach, graded from PL a to PL e, is set by the machine’s risk assessment under ISO 13849-1 (International Organization for Standardization 2023). The grip therefore bounds how fast the machine may be moving when a startled operator lets go, not which stop follows.

International Organization for Standardization. 2025a. ISO 10218-1:2025: Robotics, Safety Requirements, Part 1: Industrial Robots. International Organization for Standardization.
International Organization for Standardization. 2023. ISO 13849-1:2023: Safety of Machinery, Safety-Related Parts of Control Systems, Part 1: General Principles for Design. International Organization for Standardization.

Electromechanical enabling devices provide an additional physical condition for manual motion, one that holds even when every network packet is valid. The industrial teach pendant in figure 4 is the familiar carrier: a technician standing beside a robot uses it to jog the arm by hand, with a display, a jogging joystick, and a latched emergency-stop button on its face and an enabling grip on its rear handle. On the running machine, an enabling device belongs to a person beside the machine, such as the maintenance technician who jogs one arm joint from a pendant. The remote assistant’s console need not carry such a grip, because authenticated heartbeats maintain the manual lease, with presence assessed by the console’s chosen checks, which do not establish cognitive engagement (section 14.3.4). A site may add an enabling grip to the console as a second condition. The grip that matters is a three-position one.5 A startled or distressed operator may release a grip or squeeze it harder, and a two-position device that enables whenever pressed does not detect the latter response. By contrast, a three-position grip enables motion only in its intermediate position (Position 2). Releasing it (Position 1) or squeezing beyond the center detent (Position 3) withdraws the enabling signal; the configured safety circuit determines the resulting stop. These dual-channel contacts request the stop selected by the machine’s safety circuit, independently of the Linux application processor and its scheduler. Where that response includes Safe Torque Off (STO, IEC 61800-5-2), STO disables torque generation; the configured braking or holding mechanism must still establish the required plant response. The enabling circuit must also prevent a return through Position 2 after an over-squeeze from automatically restoring motion.

The pendant’s red mushroom button is a different device with different semantics. Under ISO 13850, the emergency stop is a complementary protective measure, not a substitute for safeguarding or for the stop the risk assessment selects. It takes priority over every other function in every operating mode, it latches until an operator deliberately resets it at the device, and that reset must not restart the machine (International Organization for Standardization 2015). Which physical response the stop invokes is a stop-category choice that Stop functions and physical response maps to its evidence. The latch and the reset rule matter for supervisory control because they make a human stop sticky in exactly the way a network command is not. A remote packet can request a stop and a lease can lapse into one, but only the local reset of a latched device, followed by the fresh admission this chapter requires, returns the machine to motion.

International Organization for Standardization. 2015. ISO 13850:2015: Safety of Machinery, Emergency Stop Function, Principles for Design. International Organization for Standardization.

Even while an operator holds an active lease and maintains Position 2 on the deadman grip, the deterministic enforcer evaluates human setpoints against the same barrier certificates, current limits, and kinematic envelopes applied to the learned neural policy. If an operator panics and commands a high-speed steering angle that would induce dynamic chassis rollover, or requests a joint torque that would shear harmonic drive gear teeth, the enforcer clamps or rejects the command. A human command therefore remains a proposal, and the third law, proposal is not permission (principle 3), binds human supervisors and neural models alike. Intervention transfers the right to propose away from the learned policy, while the right to admit stays with the permission path. With every owner’s authority bounded by a lease, what remains to design is how the right to propose changes hands, at which control tick it commits, and what the machine does when a network handover stalls.

14.3.2 Authority states and single-writer actuator ownership

If the autonomous policy and the remote console both write to a motor drive while a handover is in flight, the drive receives alternating setpoints on consecutive ticks. To prevent two sources from commanding an actuator at once, the arbiter resolves authority over each channel at every discrete time tick \(\TimeInstant\) to a single, mutually exclusive state: \[\ChannelAuthorityState_t \in \{\text{Autonomous}, \text{Handover-Pending}, \text{Blend}, \text{Human-Manual}, \text{Fallback}, \text{Protective}\}.\] These states separate proposer identity from actuator access. Each carries a short code name, used in the per-tick record and in figure 5, and each names who holds the proposal token on the channel:

  • Autonomous (AUTO): The learned model holds the proposal token and proposes setpoints, while the local enforcer verifies barrier certificates (\(\BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \ge 0\)) on every control cycle before dispatching commands.
  • Handover-Pending (PEND): A takeover handshake is negotiating across the network; the policy keeps the token and continues proposing commands under a strict clearance-derived countdown timer \(\HandoverTimeout\). Active lease and evidence expiry run concurrently with that timer, so the arbiter dispatches fallback at the earliest of the protocol, lease, evidence, and clearance deadlines.
  • Blend (BLEND, enforcer-owned bridge): No proposer holds the token. The enforcer assumes exclusive bus ownership, driving a smooth, \(\Glyph{C}^2\)-continuous quintic interpolation between the latched autonomous setpoint and the validated human target setpoint.
  • Human-Manual (MAN): The authenticated teleoperator holds the proposal token, streaming commands that pass through enforcer barrier checks under an active manual lease (\(\ManualLease\)).
  • Fallback (FALLBACK): Entered when an active lease expires, communication drops, or no admissible continuation remains. No proposer holds the token; the arbiter selects the validated HOLD or STOP rung for the measured state without waiting for remote commands. An ordinary least-change correction, by contrast, is enforcement within the current state and leaves the token with the current proposer.
  • Protective (PROTECTIVE): Entered directly from an independent hardwired protective input or a critical silicon fault. No proposer holds the token. The safety circuit selects the validated protective stop; Safe Torque Off (STO) disables torque generation, and any required load-holding mechanism must be qualified for the current state and surviving resources.

At no point do two software masters write to an actuator simultaneously. Even during the Blend state, the enforcer alone owns the actuator channel, executing a precomputed profile between the latched references and validating dynamic constraints against the current state on each tick. Each edge in table 4 therefore names its guard and deadline, its single actuator owner, and the fallback taken when the guard fails or an active lease lapses.

14.3.3 The four-phase takeover handshake

If a single network packet could switch a channel directly from Autonomous to Human-Manual, a delayed request could hand the actuators to an operator whose view of the aisle is already stale. With the same single packet, a dropped reply could leave the operator and the machine disagreeing on who is driving. Moving a channel between states instead takes a deterministic four-phase takeover protocol (Request \(\to\) Acknowledge \(\to\) Commit \(\to\) Confirm) run by the safety microcontroller. This four-stage exchange separates the local hardware commit from the physical blend and from the final release of manual proposal rights, checks cryptographic freshness so replayed frames cannot trigger a switch, and changes ownership on a single tick boundary:

  1. Notice (Request phase): Either the human supervisor notices policy drift and requests an override (for example, by pressing an enabling button or moving a joystick past its calibrated deadband), or the autonomous policy hits an epistemic uncertainty spike and requests teleoperation assistance. Upon receipt, the arbiter initializes the clearance-derived countdown timer \(\HandoverTimeout = \max(0, \min(\HandoverTimeoutCeiling, \FallbackDeadline))\) of equation 4.
  2. Declutch (Acknowledge phase): The enforcer validates target prerequisites across the system. It verifies that sensor telemetry is fresh, network links are live, and the current plant state \(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant)\) retains sufficient stopping reserve to brake safely if the transfer aborts. It then captures a synchronous state snapshot before preparing to revoke the autonomous policy’s proposal authority.
  3. Transition (Commit phase): At the next synchronous control tick boundary, the enforcer executes the state transition. It revokes the policy’s proposal token on the committed channels, latches the last approved policy setpoint alongside the checked manual target setpoint, and assigns sole write authority to the enforcer in the Blend state. The enforcer drives the \(\Glyph{C}^2\)-continuous transition profile, continuously checking that the trajectory remains feasible under measured plant dynamics.
  4. Notification (Confirm phase): The permission path broadcasts confirmation of the committed owner and the active Blend state to the operator console. A dropped confirmation packet alarms the operator interface, but does not undo or stall the local commit on the robot. After the physical profile completes under valid state, sufficient stopping reserve, and an active manual lease, the arbiter separately grants the proposal token on the committed channels to the operator and transitions to Human-Manual. This release timing is dictated by physical profile completion rather than the network notification round trip.

Each phase in table 5 carries a strictly budgeted bound, an explicit guard and owner, and deterministic failure handling. The transaction rejects spoofed override attempts and applies the failure response assigned to its current phase. Lost confirmation does not reverse a committed transfer; lease expiry or a failed physical guard invokes the validated state-dependent fallback.

14.3.4 Arbiter execution and manual-lease heartbeats

Once a channel reaches \(\text{Human-Manual}\), a dropped wireless link or a distracted assistant who leaves the console would let the last manual command keep running unless the machine checks that the operator is still present. The safety microcontroller bounds that risk with a hardware-timed manual lease. Depending on the console, presence detection may use a capacitive grip sensor, eye tracking, or continuous joystick deflection past a deadband, chosen for their sensor coverage and false-drop rates. Manual authority is sustained exclusively through authenticated keep-alive heartbeats streamed from the operator console to the safety microcontroller. The manual lease \(\ManualLease\) of section 14.3.1 is the longest silence the microcontroller tolerates between them. Two bounds set its length. From below, the lease must outlast the heartbeat period plus the link’s jitter and at least one lost packet, or ordinary wireless loss would drop a healthy operator into fallback. That floor is why the heartbeats must run at a rate several times the lease’s inverse. From above, the lease replaces the chunk lease inside \(\PrebrakeDelay\) while the operator holds MAN, so the travel it adds over the chunk lease, \(\SpeedAtDetection(\ManualLease - \ChunkLease)\), must fit the clearance left after the takeover. The reference implementation chooses 100 ms. Section 14.4.6 checks that value against the upper bound at the in-loop request speed, and only the site’s measured heartbeat loss can show that it clears the lower one.

Each heartbeat packet carries a strictly increasing 64-bit sequence counter \(\HeartbeatSequenceCounter\), a monotonic hardware timestamp \(\HeartbeatTimestamp_k\), an authenticated operator credential, and a keyed message authentication code (AES-128 CMAC, evaluated in section 14.5). In the age calculation below, \(\HeartbeatTimestamp_k\) represents the sender’s timestamp translated into the local clock domain. The receiver tracks the bounded clock-synchronization uncertainty interval and computes freshness relative to the oldest plausible timestamp; an unqualified clock mapping cannot safely establish packet freshness. The nominal local-clock age is: \[\HeartbeatAge = \HeartbeatReceiptTime - \HeartbeatTimestamp_k.\] These fields combine communication checks used in black-channel safety architectures (International Electrotechnical Commission 2021) with cryptographic security over an untrusted wireless link. The sequence counter detects transmission loss and replay attacks. The timestamp and bounded lease act as a communication watchdog against excessive latency. The cryptographic tag authenticates the claimed credential. The safety communication checks and the security mechanism require separate qualification.

International Electrotechnical Commission. 2021. IEC 61784-3:2021: Industrial Communication Networks, Profiles, Part 3: Functional Safety Fieldbuses, General Rules and Profile Definitions. International Electrotechnical Commission.

↩︎ The proposal header that applies the same black-channel safeguards to every learned proposal is established in Supervisory timers and proposal wire contracts.

A heartbeat packet may renew authority only while its conservative age falls within the configured freshness window. The active manual lease expires deterministically on the local hardware clock at its committed deadline; stale or out-of-order network traffic cannot extend that horizon. Similarly, an autonomous action chunk expires strictly according to its admitted local deadline. A packet that fails cryptographic verification is immediately discarded. It cannot renew the lease, but it does not revoke currently valid authority either; the existing timer runs down on its own. Upon lease expiration, the microcontroller automatically revokes the operator’s proposal token, drops into Fallback, and executes a pre-validated holding or stopping trajectory. Once a lease lapses, the state machine prohibits any immediate return to \(\text{Autonomous}\) or \(\text{Human-Manual}\). The robot must satisfy strict recovery conditions: it must come to a complete mechanical standstill, resolve and acknowledge the relevant faults without erasing their evidence, and undergo an authenticated four-phase re-admission handshake initiated from rest.

When multiple control sources propose concurrent commands, the permission path resolves candidate selection through a fixed priority hierarchy evaluated deterministically every 1 ms control cycle below the proposal boundary. This mechanism adopts the layered priority structure of subsumption architectures (Brooks 1986), but applies it strictly to input source selection rather than behavioral synthesis. The independent enforcer then admits, modifies, or rejects the winning candidate to ensure compliance with configured physical limits. In the \(\text{Human-Manual}\) state with a valid lease, the human proposal takes precedence over learned model proposals, while remaining bounded by barrier certificates and actuator limits. In \(\text{Autonomous}\), the learned policy generates candidate actions for safety screening. Because fixed-state arbitration requires bounded work per tick (\(\mathcal{O}(1)\) computational complexity), it eliminates runtime negotiation or dynamic bargaining between software proposers.

↩︎ The proposal boundary, above which a learned component may only propose and below which the permission path alone admits commands, is established in The Machine in Five Levels.

Two properties of the protocol show only when the states and handshake are drawn together (figure 5). Every active state has an edge to Fallback, so no failure of the network or the operator can leave a channel without a defined owner, and the only edge out of Fallback is a reset from standstill. The hardwired Protective input sits outside the protocol entirely and preempts every state.

Here the state machine decouples physical bus ownership from protocol progress. An acknowledgment prepares the transition. The local hardware commit (REVOKE_PROPOSAL_TOKEN()) strips the autonomous policy of its proposal token on the committed channels and grants the enforcer sole ownership of the blend. The enforcer admits live manual proposals only after the interpolation profile completes under an active manual lease. An unconfirmed transition therefore leaves only the console uncertain, as the Confirm phase states. A delayed handover request or a dropped heartbeat, in contrast, runs down its local timer and invokes the validated fallback without waiting for network consensus.

Assigning a single writer resolves authority unambiguously at each control tick, but an instantaneous switch can still produce hazardous dynamic transients. If the autonomous policy’s last admitted joint torque diverges significantly from the operator’s initial target, directly swapping setpoints across a single tick boundary requests an unachievable step change in torque, risking mechanical shock or actuator saturation. The enforcer avoids this discontinuity by using the latched boundary conditions to generate a dynamically feasible interpolation profile that preserves stopping margins throughout the transition.

Checkpoint 14.2: Authority transitions and timeout bounds
Before moving from who owns each channel to how its command changes value, verify your understanding of leases, transition state machines, and timeout dynamics:

If any transition paths permit unhandled timeouts or undefined intermediate states, the authority protocol is vulnerable to mode confusion and runaway actuation. Review this section before proceeding to smooth command blending.

14.4 Transfer Without Discontinuity

At commit time \(\TimeInstant_0\), the enforcer latches the last admitted policy command \(\ControlCommand_0\) and a checked human target \(\ControlCommand_1\). Switching them in one permission tick may exceed the channel’s allowed command rate. In classical process control, bumpless transfer between manual and automatic modes is achieved by making the inactive controller track the active one’s output, so its internal state, including its integrator, already agrees at the switch (Åström and Hägglund 2006). Section 14.4.3 applies that tracking to the console-side controller that turns operator input into a command. Here bumpless transfer also names a second mechanism layered on top, which spreads the remaining command change across a finite window while matching the required endpoint derivatives. The quintic seam bridges of Quintic splines and C^2 boundary matching provide the construction; takeover adds a new admission decision because waiting for the operator has already consumed some clearance.

14.4.1 Command steps and mechanical drivetrain shock

A step change in torque or acceleration excites torsional resonances and snaps gearboxes across their backlash, while a slower blend lowers the command rate at the cost of stopping reserve. Because the \(\PowerRailInductance\,d\ConductorCurrent/d\TimeInstant\) coupling to the DC supply also turns a current step into a voltage sag on the shared rail, the command transition must satisfy the drive’s rate limits while leaving enough clearance for fallback. A smooth profile therefore needs both a rate bound and a check against the remaining physical envelope.

↩︎ The \(\PowerRailInductance\,d\ConductorCurrent/d\TimeInstant\) coupling through which a current step sags the shared DC bus is established in Power distribution impedance, transient droop, and brownout resets.

Definition 14.2: C² bumpless authority transfer ramp

\(\Glyph{C}^2\) bumpless authority transfer ramp is the enforcer-owned command profile between two checked, latched references \(\ControlCommand_0\) and \(\ControlCommand_1\). It is parameterized by a monotonic weighting function \(\BumplessTransferBlend(\TimeInstant) \in [0,1]\) with vanishing first and second boundary derivatives (\(\BumplessTransferBlendRate = \BumplessTransferBlendAcceleration = 0\)) over a finite blending window \(\BlendingTransitionWindow\). The profile keeps that command and its first two derivatives continuous across the fixed-endpoint handoff. When the command is acceleration, its first derivative is commanded jerk; when it is torque, the first derivative is torque rate.

  1. Significance: A setpoint step has an unbounded ideal derivative. A \(\Glyph{C}^2\) command profile removes that discontinuity; measured actuator tracking and plant limits still decide physical feasibility.
  2. Distinction: Unlike a linear interpolation ramp whose derivative jumps at both endpoints, a \(\Glyph{C}^2\) quintic spline enforces vanishing first and second derivatives (\(\BumplessTransferBlendRate = \BumplessTransferBlendAcceleration = 0\)) at entry and exit, making the interpolated command and its first two derivatives continuous for stationary endpoint derivatives. The physical meaning of those derivatives depends on the command channel.
  3. Common pitfall: Choosing a window shorter than the rate limit allows for a fixed acceleration-command change (the bound equation 7 derives below) breaches the stated command-jerk limit even though the profile is smooth. Resonance, slip, and stopping distance require plant tests.

14.4.2 Sizing the blend window and the stopping-distance cost

Smoothing an acceleration command from cruising speed to emergency braking delays the onset of maximum deceleration, so bumpless transfer consumes clearance. It trades stopping reserve for lower mechanical stress, and sizing the blend window means balancing the actuator’s jerk limit against the clearance that remains.

The enforcer blends with the quintic smoothstep. For normalized progress \(\BumplessTransferProgress = (\TimeInstant - \BumplessTransferStartInstant) / \BlendingTransitionWindow \in [0, 1]\), the polynomial weighting function is: \[\BumplessTransferBlend(\BumplessTransferProgress) = 10\BumplessTransferProgress^3 - 15\BumplessTransferProgress^4 + 6\BumplessTransferProgress^5. \tag{6}\] The polynomial in equation 6 is the fixed-endpoint result derived in Smooth Trajectories and Bumpless Transfer. Its derivative \(\BumplessTransferBlendSlope(\BumplessTransferProgress) = 30\BumplessTransferProgress^2(1-\BumplessTransferProgress)^2\) peaks at \(\BumplessTransferProgress = 1/2\) with value \(30/16 = 1.875\), so the profile’s peak slope is \(1.875/\BlendingTransitionWindow\) and an acceleration change must satisfy: \[\BlendingTransitionWindow \ge \frac{1.875 \lVert \FiniteChange \ControlCommand \rVert}{\JerkLimit}. \tag{7}\]

For a torque command, replace the acceleration difference and jerk ceiling with the torque difference and torque-rate ceiling. The illustrative 15 N·m joint-torque change under a 750 N·m/s ceiling therefore requires 37.5 ms, rounded up to 38 ms on the permission clock.
