These bounds assume stationary endpoint derivatives; a transfer from a moving ramp must match its boundary state explicitly.6

6 Nonzero Boundary Derivatives: The \(1.875/\BlendingTransitionWindow\) peak derivative factor assumes stationary endpoint derivatives. A general quintic can match the command value, first derivative, and second derivative at both endpoints, including nonzero values (Quintic splines and C^2 boundary matching). Its interior rate extrema must then be checked; the fixed-endpoint smoothstep factor does not automatically apply.

Increasing \(\BlendingTransitionWindow\) reduces the profile’s peak commanded jerk, but delays the full human target and can lengthen the stopping trajectory. Consider the remote assistant’s takeover of the mobile manipulator’s base, committed while the machine cruises at the \(\SpeedAtDetection =\) 0.75 m/s it slowed to before requesting it (section 14.2.4). Suppose the assistant commands a stop at the scenario’s braking deceleration, a fixed step of \(\lvert \FiniteChange \BaseAccelerationCommand \rvert =\) 2 m/s² from the latched cruise command. The enforcer holds the blend to \(\JerkLimit =\) 7.11 m/s³, the jerk bound of the resident jerk-limited stop. That bound is a stipulated property of the loaded base, not of its speed, so the blend window does not depend on the speed at which the takeover commits. Measured load response still requires qualification. The smoothstep bound then sets a minimum blending window of 527.34 ms, longer than the 281.25 ms the resident stop spends ramping its deceleration at the same jerk bound, because the smoothstep reaches its peak slope only at mid-window. The smoothstep weight averages one half over the window, so the base would leave the blend at 0.22 m/s after traveling 316.1 mm. Integrating the smoothstep weight a first time subtracts one half of \(\lvert \FiniteChange \BaseAccelerationCommand \rvert\,\BlendingTransitionWindow\) from the entry speed \(\SpeedAtDetection\), whereas integrating that speed profile across the window subtracts only one seventh of \(\lvert \FiniteChange \BaseAccelerationCommand \rvert\,\BlendingTransitionWindow^2\) from the unbraked travel \(\SpeedAtDetection\,\BlendingTransitionWindow\) because most of the deceleration occurs in the second half of the ramp. Braking from there at the full target would take another 12.4 mm, for 328.4 mm to zero speed in this partial model. It holds full deceleration until rest, then switches acceleration to zero instantaneously. The value is therefore a lower-bound comparison for the ramp followed by ideal braking, not a complete smooth stopping trajectory. A feasible terminal transition needs its own distance and boundary-state check.

An instantaneous step takeover would stop in 140.6 mm, so even this partial ramp-and-brake model adds 187.8 mm of travel. The partial model is also 82.4 mm longer than the 246.1 mm resident stop from the same speed. For the one-dimensional model starting at zero acceleration and ending at rest with zero acceleration, the resident profile is a minimum-distance stop under the stated deceleration and jerk bounds (The C^2 stopping suffix and jerk-limited braking), so a different complete transition into braking must be checked against that baseline. The partial comparison above lacks the required terminal transition and is not itself a competing complete profile. For a braking command the resident stop is already the bumpless transfer. The base’s blend window therefore governs base command changes that do not stop the machine, such as the first manual command after an automatic stop, where the blend starts from rest and the enforcer still checks the resident stop from every blended state. An idealized step stop is mathematically shorter. Executing an unblended emergency stop, however, risks wheel lockup, dynamic rollover, or load shedding (such as throwing a loaded tote off the manipulator rack), which can increase the actual physical stopping distance beyond the ideal theoretical rigid-body stop. Command smoothing limits the requested rate; physical stability and sufficient clearance still require qualification. The enforcer admits each blended tick only while the resident stop from the resulting state still fits. Where no blend fits, the base receives the resident stop itself, the suffix the fallback ladder’s stop rung executes. Bypassing the blend window by applying the acceleration step within a single 1 ms permission tick would instead request a command jerk of about 2,000 m/s³ over that tick. The idealized step is only a comparison baseline.

Under the stated stopping model, the blend adds travel beyond the resident stop.

14.4.3 Initial condition matching and integrator pre-biasing

Command blending removes the chosen output discontinuity, but it cannot remove one that a controller upstream of the blend produces. On the remote assistant’s side that controller is the console-side tracking loop, the software that turns joystick deflection, or a measured guiding force (the force-guided input that section 14.4.4 describes next), into a velocity target for the channel. While the policy still owns the channel, that loop runs against a plant it does not drive, and its integrator accumulates the difference between its target and a motion that never follows. At commit it would emit that accumulated correction as a jump in the manual target, a step the blend window was never sized to absorb. Initial condition matching pre-biases that loop against the measured state before commit, so its internal controller state matches the output required at transfer rather than releasing accumulated error. It does not force the deliberate human target to equal the last policy command. The enforcer latches both endpoints, sizes the blend for their actual difference, and checks the resulting command and stopping reserve throughout transfer.7

7 Mid-Blend Re-Intervention Abort: If an operator commands a secondary protective stop while a bumpless transfer blend is actively underway (\(0 < \BumplessTransferProgress < 1\)), the enforcer does not wait for \(\BlendingTransitionWindow\) to complete. It uses the measured plant position, velocity, and acceleration, together with the active command and their uncertainty, to select a qualified state-matched fallback. A new polynomial seeded only from the intermediate command does not establish that the physical plant can execute it.

To verify a transfer, record the command profile, motor current, encoder motion, and chassis inertial response on a shared timebase with bounded alignment error. In the ideal model, a step command has an infinite derivative at the switch, which excites high jerk, unmodeled compliance, and drivetrain ringing in physical hardware. A fixed-endpoint quintic polynomial removes the command discontinuity by zeroing the boundary derivatives, so its command rate stays bounded across the whole window \(\BlendingTransitionWindow\) instead of concentrating in one impulse (figure 6). The measured plant response, not the command profile, establishes whether the handover met its physical limits.

The two panels distinguish the command’s mathematical continuity from the plant’s measured response. Removing the step bounds the requested rate under the stated endpoint assumptions; it does not establish that the drive tracks the command or that the load remains stable. Those measurements decide whether the selected blend window is acceptable.

The enforcer’s 1 kHz loop gathers the four-phase handshake, integrator pre-biasing, and the \(\Glyph{C}^2\) quintic blend into one protocol on the safety microcontroller. Each tick it samples plant state, stopping reserve, protective inputs, and the active execution lease. It commits an ownership change only on the qualified commit tick that follows a verified acknowledgment, blends only between latched endpoints and checks every blended candidate before dispatch, and falls back on a lapsed lease or a failed clearance check.

↪ Protocol: The enforcer’s cadence, memory and write invariants, and per-state execution steps are given in Authority-transfer protocol.

14.4.4 Mapping operator input to arm motion

Once the operator holds the arm channel, commanding a rigid position target into a bin wall or rack upright builds contact force until the enforcer trips, whereas guiding the arm through measured contact force lets the wrist comply. Position streaming supplies kinematic targets directly; force guiding uses measured contact force to generate a motion target through an admittance controller8 (Hogan 1985). Either mapping remains subject to the arm’s qualified limits. The choice also changes the logged learning target, so the record must retain whether a sample is a requested pose, a force measurement, or an admitted command.

8 Admittance and Impedance Control: Hogan (1985) framed interaction control as programming the relation between force and motion at the contact. An impedance controller answers imposed motion with a commanded force; an admittance controller is its dual, turning a measured force into commanded motion, which suits stiff, highly geared arms whose position loops are accurate but which cannot render force directly. On such an arm a force-guided target is still a motion command, so it passes the same enforcer checks as a streamed pose.

Hogan, Neville. 1985. “Impedance Control: An Approach to Manipulation: Part i–Theory, Part II–Implementation, Part III–Applications.” Journal of Dynamic Systems, Measurement, and Control 107 (1): 1–24.

14.4.5 Bilateral teleoperation and haptic feedback passivity

A force-reflecting console adds an actuated device at the operator’s hands. Delayed feedback9 can destabilize that coupled loop even when authority ownership is correct (Anderson and Spong 1989). Such a console therefore needs a separate interaction-energy argument, including its port conventions, controller sampling, delay and loss assumptions, and actuator saturation. The energy-storage condition and wave-variable construction are developed in Passivity and Energy-Bounded Interaction; they do not replace the clearance or force limits. The basic warehouse takeover does not require bilateral force reflection. If a site adds it, pre-biasing and ramping the console’s force output must prevent a command step at the operator’s hand within its own measured limits, because the arm’s accepted blend duration does not automatically qualify the console. Whichever input mapping and console a site chooses, the session that carries them must still fit the clearance left after the operator’s response.

9 Delayed Teleoperation: Ferrell (1965) found that operators working through a transmission delay fall back on a move-and-wait strategy, so task time grows with the delay. Force reflection through the same delay proved unstable until Anderson and Spong (1989) used scattering theory to make the communication channel passive, a construction that Niemeyer and Slotine (1991) recast as wave variables. For a force-reflecting console the delay budget is therefore a stability term as well as a latency term.

Ferrell, William R. 1965. “Remote Manipulation with Transmission Delay.” IEEE Transactions on Human Factors in Electronics HFE-6 (1): 24–32. https://doi.org/10.1109/THFE.1965.6591253.
Anderson, Robert J., and Mark W. Spong. 1989. “Bilateral Control of Teleoperators with Time Delay.” IEEE Transactions on Automatic Control 34 (5): 494–501. https://doi.org/10.1109/9.24201.
Niemeyer, Günter, and Jean-Jacques E. Slotine. 1991. “Stable Adaptive Teleoperation.” IEEE Journal of Oceanic Engineering 16 (1): 152–62. https://doi.org/10.1109/48.64895.

14.4.6 Admitting the remote-assistant session

Every interval priced so far in this chapter now draws from the same finite stopping reserve when a remote session begins. Admitting the remote assistant requires bounding four timing parameters against the machine’s limits: the operator reaction budget (the takeover latency \(\SupervisoryTakeoverLatency\) of equation 1 for the takeover mode in use), the handshake protocol latency \(\HandshakeProtocolLatency\), the manual lease \(\ManualLease\), and the minimum blending window \(\BlendingTransitionWindow\). Table 6 states them for the machine’s two command channels, the base and the arm, which one remote-assistant session commands together.

↪ How many machines one remote assistant can serve before requests wait minutes for a person is developed in One person, many machines.

Consider an illustrative remote-assistant in-loop takeover, requested after the mobile manipulator has slowed to 0.75 m/s with the assistant’s manual_drive entry. The four parameters take the values already derived. The reaction budget \(\SupervisoryTakeoverLatency\) is the 605 ms in-loop takeover of section 14.2.2, which already includes transport and the 31 ms four-phase exchange of table 5, so the handshake is part of \(\SupervisoryTakeoverLatency\), not an addition to it. The manual lease, the longest silence tolerated from the assistant before the permission path falls back, is 100 ms, and table 6 gives the blending window for each channel.

An in-loop session at 0.75 m/s closes its complete budget by only a few centimeters, and only because the base takes its resident stop rather than a braking blend. A session that begins at the 0.15 m/s crawl or from standstill has far more room, so those are the configurations worth qualifying. Table 7 charges the in-loop session’s intervals one at a time against the clearance the finished budget leaves at that speed.

Three rows of the table decide that verdict. The remainder after the takeover, the 146.6 ms by which the takeover beats \(\FallbackDeadline\) (section 14.2.4), bounds the transfer itself. Any further delay before a stop could begin while authority is changing hands must fit inside it, and the enforcer checks the resident stop against it at every tick of the transfer. The lease row exists because, once the assistant holds MAN, the manual lease replaces the 60 ms chunk lease inside \(\PrebrakeDelay\), so its extra length becomes travel. The row is the upper bound on the lease set out in section 14.3.4, and the chosen value passes it. The blend row fails. Blending the step to braking from 0.75 m/s grows the travel plus the resident stop from the blended state by up to 157.0 mm, well past the remainder, so in this session the base receives the resident stop and only the arm blends. This conservative per-tick check charges a fresh resident stop starting from zero deceleration at each blended speed rather than the partial model’s 82.4 mm constant-deceleration tail, because any fallback triggered during the blend begins from the standard resident profile. The slow-down was derived with \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) charged, so the complete budget closes, but by centimeters, another reason the base should complete its stop before accepting continued manual motion.

At the crawl, after a 4.5 s out-of-loop takeover, 198.4 mm remains before the same charges, and the base’s stop from the crawl is its resident stop, with no braking blend to reserve. The admission allocation and the lease extension cost travel in proportion to speed, so at the crawl they take only a small part of that remainder. Once the assistant holds MAN, the live check of table 3 uses the current remaining clearance, the measured speed, and only the unelapsed portions of those intervals; commit does not restore the distance spent waiting or blending. These figures screen the candidates. Qualification must still check the rounded executable profile and the state-matched fallback, including delay travel, over the full transition, against a measured \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\).

Every command in that session, whether a streamed pose, a force-guided target, or a heartbeat that renews the manual lease, must also come from the authenticated operator that the manual_drive entry names. A budget that closes by a few centimeters leaves no room for a forged or replayed packet, which makes identity and packet admission the next constraint.

14.5 Authenticating an Intervention

On a shared wireless link, a legitimate veto_path intervention packet and an unauthorized drive-torque packet can arrive within the very same control cycle. The permission architecture must prevent an adversary or malformed network frame from seizing kinetic authority, yet it must never obstruct an authorized operator from arresting the machine before a collision. Systems engineers reconcile the two demands by classifying intervention operations according to the mechanical work they produce. Stopping the machine by removing power or engaging a mechanical holding brake is not symmetrical with injecting active motion commands, so the two actions take different verification paths. A protective stop brings the machine to a controlled halt along a braking or holding rung of the fallback ladder rather than abruptly cutting actuator torque. Commanding base velocities, wheel torques, or joint trajectories, by contrast, injects fresh mechanical energy into the plant: a forged or corrupted trajectory command can drive a moving mass into an obstacle, whereas an authenticated out-of-band protective input only requests a plant-specific minimum-risk stop.

Authentication adds delay to the remote intervention path, so it is priced against the same deadline as every other latency source, the latest fallback deadline \(\FallbackDeadline\) of equation 4. At the warehouse aisle velocity of 1.3 m/s, the partial spare permits at most 100.8 ms of added delay before charging the admission latency \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\), which drops to 37.5 ms once the placement allocation is charged. Transferring published Cortex-M4 cycle counts to the reference microcontroller clock gives an illustrative estimate of 1.8 ms for one Ed25519 signature verification10 (Owens et al. 2024), which translates into 2.34 mm of physical travel and leaves 128.7 mm of spare displacement margin. The same cycle-count and clock-rate estimate for an AES-128 CMAC over one command frame is 5.0 μs (Dworkin 2005; Schwabe and Stoffelen 2017). Per packet, both estimates fit within the deadline; loaded measurements on the target must qualify them. The design problem is therefore not choosing an algorithm but meeting five requirements on the admission path that carries the packets:

10 Ed25519: The Edwards-curve signature scheme of Bernstein et al. (2012) provides rapid verification alongside compact 32-byte public keys and 64-byte signatures. Constant-time implementations eliminate secret-dependent branches and memory lookups to protect against side-channel timing attacks. While that defense primarily shields the signing teleoperation console, the receiving safety microcontroller only verifies incoming frames. Well-formed invalid signatures can still require the expensive verification path, even when malformed inputs admit early rejection. The verifier’s processing load therefore depends on admitted traffic and its verification paths, the vulnerability that the verifier-flooding analysis of section 14.9 prices.

Bernstein, Daniel J., Niels Duif, Tanja Lange, Peter Schwabe, and Bo-Yin Yang. 2012. “High-Speed High-Security Signatures.” Journal of Cryptographic Engineering 2 (2): 77–89. https://doi.org/10.1007/s13389-012-0027-1.
Owens, Daniel, Rabih El Khatib, Mojtaba Bisheh-Niasar, Reza Azarderakhsh, and Mehran Mozaffari Kermani. 2024. “Efficient and Side-Channel Resistant Ed25519 on ARM Cortex-M4.” IEEE Transactions on Circuits and Systems I: Regular Papers 71 (6): 2674–86. https://doi.org/10.1109/TCSI.2024.3384414.
Dworkin, Morris J. 2005. Recommendation for Block Cipher Modes of Operation: The CMAC Mode for Authentication. NIST Special Publication 800-38B. National Institute of Standards; Technology. https://doi.org/10.6028/NIST.SP.800-38B.
Schwabe, Peter, and Ko Stoffelen. 2017. “All the AES You Need on Cortex-M3 and M4.” Selected Areas in Cryptography (SAC 2016), Lecture notes in computer science, vol. 10532: 180–94. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-69453-5_10.
  1. Protective stops never wait for verification. The local hardware fallback timer and independently wired emergency inputs act unconditionally, regardless of whether cryptographic routines have completed or stalled. If a verification routine overruns its deadline, the permission subsystem marks remote assistance as unavailable and cedes control to the autonomous protection layer; it never postpones an emergency deceleration. As analyzed in the verifier-flooding pitfall of section 14.9, what causes a cryptographic verifier to miss real-time deadlines is rarely the algorithmic cost of an individual packet, but rather the ingress volume of hostile or unvetted network traffic saturating the execution queue.
  2. Takeover requests must be attributable to an individual. Because a symmetric Message Authentication Code (MAC) relies on a shared key known to both endpoints, a valid tag proves frame integrity but cannot prove which endpoint originated the transmission. An asymmetric digital signature, however, cryptographically binds the takeover request to the teleoperator’s private key, supporting attribution when credential enrollment, key custody, and compromise evidence also identify the operator. The architecture therefore mandates an asymmetric digital signature on the session-initiating manual_drive request, while subsequent streaming commands and periodic heartbeats rely on the lower-overhead symmetric MAC.
  3. Freshness represents a spatial displacement budget, not merely a clock tolerance. In embodied systems, packet latency translates directly into unmodeled vehicle travel. A replayed or delayed motion packet arriving at the boundary of a 100 ms freshness acceptance window commands an action based on an obsolete vehicle pose. Over that interval the mobile base travels 130 mm, consuming nearly the entire safety margin and leaving only 1.0 mm of the 131.0 mm partial spare. Once the admission latency \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) is factored in, such a replay completely overruns the available spare, so the allowable freshness window must shrink below 37.5 ms. The receiver drops counters no newer than the last authenticated, accepted counter before spending processor cycles on cryptography. It advances that stored counter only after authentication and freshness checks pass, so an unauthenticated high counter cannot invalidate later legitimate traffic. Furthermore, if heartbeat packets cease for longer than the lease duration \(\ManualLease\), the controller immediately revokes manual authority and engages the certified local fallback ladder.
  4. Malformed traffic never alters control authority. Any packet with a corrupted checksum, an out-of-order sequence counter, or an invalid authentication tag is immediately rejected and recorded according to Rule A4 of section 14.8. A corrupted packet neither modifies existing actuator channel assignments nor triggers a spurious emergency stop. Rejecting an isolated invalid packet preserves still-valid authority; interference or dropouts that prevent timely renewal still expire the lease and invoke the qualified fallback.
  5. Every actuator-write path must be accounted for. All side channels capable of driving physical hardware, including diagnostic ports, hardware bootloaders, debug interfaces, and direct fieldbus wiring, must be gated by the hardware permission authority or guarded by dedicated interlocks. The intervention threat model explicitly accounts for unauthorized remote packets injected across wireless links, replay attacks traversing internal communication buses, and rogue commands originating from a compromised high-level application processor. Physical tampering, by contrast, enters the safety assurance case only when explicitly mandated by the operational threat model.

These authentication and transport latencies directly impact policy learning and sim-to-real transfer. An embodied simulator that provides instantaneous human takeover underprices the danger of visiting marginal states. If an autonomous policy learns that intervention occurs instantaneously, it will tolerate trajectories that are physically unrecoverable once realistic communication, cryptographic verification, and actuator fallback latencies are introduced. Training and hardware-in-the-loop evaluation pipelines must therefore inject these bounded authentication, transport, and mechanical reaction delays.

Verifying an incoming packet authenticates its credential; the capability manifest separately determines its privileges. Cryptographic validity alone cannot guarantee dynamic stability during control transfer. When authority shifts while the chassis carries linear momentum and the manipulator actuators exert active joint torques, the permission path must commit one selected source per channel and announce that ownership to the operator. Local mutual exclusion and human mode awareness are separate requirements; a valid packet establishes neither by itself.

14.6 Authority Handover Failure Modes

When the warehouse mobile manipulator’s perception policy meets an unmodeled specular reflection from wet shrink-wrap on a pallet, depth artifacts drop its confidence and the runtime asks a remote assistant to reposition the gripper. If the console grants manual joystick authority over the arm without locking the base channel or announcing that the base is still moving, an assistant focused on aligning the fingers assumes the chassis is stationary. Meanwhile the autonomous navigation thread keeps driving the wheels into a rack upright. Every individual hardware and software component in that handover operates within its specification. The collision comes from mode confusion, the failure class that Sarter and Woods identified in automated aircraft cockpits (Sarter and Woods 1995). Every operating mode added to a control loop multiplies the state a human supervisor must track. When an interface changes modes without unambiguous telemetry of which agent holds each channel, operators take actions that are right for their assumed mode and hazardous in the actual one (Sarter and Woods 1995). In embodied physical systems, the most dangerous form of this failure is authority ambiguity: a state in which the autonomous runtime assumes the human has taken over a physical degree of freedom, while the human assumes the automated controller still guides it.

Definition 14.3: Mode confusion

Mode confusion is a mismatch between the operational state a human supervisor believes an automated system occupies and its actual execution mode, leading the supervisor to take actions that would be correct for the presumed state but are hazardous in the real one.

  1. Significance: When control channels transfer authority independently, an operator’s mental model of one channel can easily go stale while their attention is consumed by another. For example, an operator dedicates the 605 ms in-loop response window entirely to an arm alignment, under the false belief that the base is parked. The base, running under the autonomous policy at 1.3 m/s aisle speed, then travels 786.5 mm, which is 16.1× the 48.7 mm margin the complete budget leaves for \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\).
  2. Distinction: Conflicting command streams between concurrent software modules represent an arbitration fault, which single-owner bus gating resolves by construction. Mode confusion, by contrast, resides within the supervisor’s mental model of the machine. Resolving it requires committed channel masks and explicit per-channel status annunciation at the console, because deterministic ownership on the CAN bus or Ethernet backplane cannot correct a human’s false assumptions.
  3. Common pitfall: Telemetry confirming that an operator’s gaze is directed at a primary task monitor does not guarantee the absence of mode confusion. Tracking an operator’s visual focus onto an end-effector camera stream reveals nothing about whether that operator realizes the autonomous navigation stack still holds kinetic authority over the drive chassis.

On a machine with several axes, control authority splits naturally across distinct groups of joints, such as the mobile base’s planar velocity (\(\BaseLongitudinalVelocity, \BaseLateralVelocity, \BaseHeadingRate\)) and the manipulator arm’s joint positions. In the wet-shrink-wrap collision, those channels transferred independently. Telemetry logs reveal the fault as disjointed control flags toggling asynchronously across execution threads. At the human-machine interface, however, the split state stays invisible until physical clearance is exhausted, because neither the policy nor the human receives an immediate, unambiguous indicator of the other’s partial withdrawal.

To prevent this desynchronization, handover protocols must enforce explicit, per-channel bitmasks across every phase of negotiation. Every Request, Acknowledge, and Commit message must declare the exact subset of degrees of freedom being transferred. The formal transition state machine of section 14.3 moves proposal tokens exclusively for those designated channels, and the subsequent Confirm phase explicitly annunciates active ownership on each channel back to the operator console. A channel left out of the bitmask may keep its autonomous owner, so the base can keep navigating while the operator takes the arm. This holds only while the console annunciates that the base is still moving under the policy and the operator’s acknowledgment covered that state. Without that annunciation, the commit places the untransferred channel in a certified holding state, such as an active brake hold, rather than letting autonomous execution continue silently. The wet-shrink-wrap collision above is the second rule’s case, an untransferred base left running without annunciation.

Explicit channel ownership protocols solve arbitration on the digital bus, but they cannot restore the situational awareness of an out-of-loop human. For this reason, the in-loop and out-of-loop takeovers of section 14.2.2 must be treated as distinct operational states with different timing budgets, the 605 ms and 4.5 s that set different request speeds. While gaze tracking can verify where an operator directs their eyes, it cannot verify cognitive comprehension or justify truncating the required takeover latency budget. Local stopping envelopes must remain continuously armed and active, even when the console reports an attentive supervisor. Two field incidents show what happens when either half of this two-layer protection is missing.

In the Tempe, Arizona collision, the National Transportation Safety Board (NTSB) determined that the safety driver was visually distracted by a personal smartphone, identifying operator complacency and inadequate monitoring policies as primary contributing factors (National Transportation Safety Board 2019). The vehicle architecture had disabled automatic emergency braking during testing and relied entirely on the human supervisor to perceive hazards and intervene. In the San Francisco Cruise incident, an autonomous vehicle executed an emergency stop after striking a pedestrian who had been thrown into its path by an adjacent human-driven car. The onboard planner, however, subsequently initiated an automated pull-over maneuver, dragging the pedestrian beneath the chassis (Quinn Emanuel Urquhart and Sullivan, LLP 2024). The Tempe disaster demonstrates why an operator-monitoring contract must always be backed by an autonomous, local stopping envelope capable of bringing the machine to a halt within the remaining sensor clearance, regardless of operator attentiveness. The Cruise incident demonstrates that unexpected physical contact, tactile sensor anomalies, or sudden external force spikes must immediately lock out all motion authority into a verified physical inspection hold, rather than allowing automated post-collision clearing routines to execute.

National Transportation Safety Board. 2019. Collision Between Vehicle Controlled by Developmental Automated Driving System and Pedestrian, Tempe, Arizona, March 18, 2018. HAR-19/03. National Transportation Safety Board.
Quinn Emanuel Urquhart and Sullivan, LLP. 2024. Report to the Boards of Directors of Cruise LLC, GM Cruise Holdings LLC, and General Motors Holdings LLC Regarding the October 2, 2023 Accident in San Francisco. Quinn Emanuel Urquhart; Sullivan, LLP.

↩︎ The Tempe timeline, including the action suppression that withheld emergency braking, is established in When the Responsibilities Fail Together.

A human operator’s manual command can oppose an active safety barrier without creating a multi-writer conflict on the actuator bus. When an operator commands a manipulator toward a prohibited obstacle, the downstream safety enforcer either projects the trajectory onto the safe half-space or rejects the command entirely, dispatching only the admitted control action to the motor drives. However, the supervisory console must make this active constraint immediately visible to the human. If the interface conceals why the robot has stopped responding, the operator will instinctively command higher control effort or larger joystick displacements, leading to severe command saturation and instability once the constraint clears. Furthermore, when the console incorporates bilateral teleoperation or force-feedback interfaces, system stability depends directly on the passivity-based energy bounds established in section 14.4.5; ownership tokens alone cannot prevent feedback oscillations across a physical-virtual coupled loop.

Authority ambiguity also corrupts downstream imitation learning. If the record cannot say who held each channel, interventions are mislabeled as autonomous behavior or the reverse, and a behavioral-cloning policy learns to reproduce the very errors that triggered the takeover. A low offline validation loss cannot catch this, because it only shows that the policy memorized the wrong labels. Causal intervention masking, developed in section 14.8.3, depends on ownership bits that the authority record stamps at commit time.

War Story 14.1: Ground-contact interlocks on flight 2904

Context: On Lufthansa Flight 2904 at Warsaw, the flight crew executed an approach based on outdated wind reports, encountering an unreported tailwind that caused the aircraft to touch down at high speed far down a waterlogged runway. Upon touchdown, the crew immediately commanded maximum manual braking and deployed thrust reversers, but the aircraft’s automated flight-control logic refused to grant deceleration authority (Main Commission Aircraft Accident Investigation Warsaw and German Federal Bureau of Aircraft Accident Investigation 1994).

Mechanism: Crosswinds and runway hydroplaning delayed the left gear compression: the right main gear made contact 9 s before the left. The aircraft’s deceleration interlocks were rigid. Ground spoiler deployment required weight-on-wheels shock-strut compression on both main gears or wheel spin-up above 72 knots. Thrust reversers required weight compression on both main struts. Mechanical wheel braking was locked out until the main wheels achieved synchronized spin-up. Because hydroplaning prevented wheel spin-up and an asymmetric roll angle kept the left strut uncompressed, the control logic determined the aircraft was still airborne. Consequently, the flight computer locked out spoilers and reversers for 9 s and inhibited wheel brakes for even longer.

Impact: Wheel braking was inhibited until 13 s after initial touchdown. With braking authority locked out across the primary deceleration zone, the aircraft could not dissipate its kinetic energy along the remaining wet runway. It overran the runway threshold at 72 knots, struck an airport embankment, and caught fire, resulting in two fatalities and total hull loss.

Response: Official accident investigators cited the ground-contact interlock logic as a critical contributing cause alongside crew decision-making and delayed meteorological updates. The crew could not override the spoiler and reverser lockouts. The report identifies those design constraints alongside crew actions and incomplete landing-distance guidance; it does not establish that a manual override alone would have prevented the overrun.

Systems lesson: Embodied robots share this exact failure topology whenever automated sensor preconditions, such as docking latch checks, wheel-slip traction observers, or ground-contact switches, gate emergency braking or reverse-torque commands issued by human supervisors. Unmodeled wheel slippage or uneven terrain can violate a sensor predicate precisely when deceleration is urgently required. Any control architecture that gates physical actuation behind automated preconditions must satisfy three rules. First, it must immediately annunciate unsatisfied interlocks across the operator console. Second, it must explicitly identify the unfulfilled sensor predicate. Third, it must provide a deterministic fallback mode for sensor disagreement. Rather than bypassing safety interlocks blindly, the supervisory architecture must define a certified fallback deceleration path that executes based on conservative, physically verifiable stops.

Main Commission Aircraft Accident Investigation Warsaw and German Federal Bureau of Aircraft Accident Investigation. 1994. Report on the Accident to Airbus A320-211 Aircraft in Warsaw on 14 September 1993. Polish Ministry of Transport; Maritime Economy / BFU.

The formal authority arbiter detailed in section 14.3 exists to prevent these failures. Safe embodied control architectures cannot rely on implicit authority handoffs, assumed human vigilance, or ad hoc torque summing on the actuator bus. Evaluating post-incident behavior requires a tamper-evident, channel-by-channel telemetry record captured at every control cycle, with explicit gaps and bounded event timing. The surviving record can help distinguish a safety boundary breach, an invalid token exchange, and electrical noise on a safety bus only to the extent that its independent measurements, capture coverage, and timing bounds separate those events. Its storage path must therefore survive unexpected power interruptions, kernel panics, and real-time scheduling stalls.

14.7 Trustworthy Operational Logs

Consider a robotic arm striking a warehouse racking upright during a supervisory takeover. If the operational log contains an unrecorded gap, investigators cannot determine whether the policy proposed the damaging trajectory, the enforcer admitted it, or the operator received authority too late to intervene. Rigorous incident reconstruction requires two foundational properties: declared loss handling and an operational record engineered to survive physical failure.

A trustworthy record must capture state on both sides of the causal boundary, paired with independent plant observations.11 Motor phase currents, optical encoder positions, and wrist force-torque measurements provide physical observations distinct from software setpoints, subject to their calibration, bandwidth, and uncertainty bounds. Dispatched commands alone cannot prove delivered actuator torque or establish mechanical collision causality.

11 Event Data Recorder Baseline: A passenger-vehicle event data recorder captures 5 s of pre-crash data at 2 Hz (phasing in from September 2028 to 20 s at 10 Hz), which even under the modernized rule yields only one sample per 100 ms across 100 permission ticks (National Highway Traffic Safety Administration 2024). Similarly, the data storage system mandated for automated lane-keeping systems under UN Regulation No. 157 logs system activations, overrides, and transition demands to establish driver attribution, but its timestamps require accuracy only within 1 s, or 1,000 permission ticks (United Nations Economic Commission for Europe 2021). Because neither regulatory standard provides the time resolution needed to order an intervention against physical contact within a single control cycle, the autonomous machine must maintain its own deterministic, per-tick operational record.

National Highway Traffic Safety Administration. 2024. Event Data Recorders: Final Rule (49 CFR Part 563). 89 FR 102810. U.S. Department of Transportation.
United Nations Economic Commission for Europe. 2021. UN Regulation No. 157: Uniform Provisions Concerning the Approval of Vehicles with Regard to Automated Lane Keeping Systems. E/ECE/TRANS/505/Rev.3/Add.156. United Nations Economic Commission for Europe.

↩︎ A record of what was proposed, evaluated, dispatched, and measured is the third demand on the permission path established in Where Safety, Security, and Accountability Meet.

At every cycle, the 1 kHz deterministic control loop records a structured authority and action tuple under declared loss handling. The minimal evidence tuple takes the form: \[\langle \MicrocontrollerClockTime, \RecordedAuthorityState, \MechanicalPlantState, \NominalControlCommand, \CertifiedControlAction, \JointTorque, \text{ID}_{\text{operator}}, \RemainingExecutionLease\rangle\] This tuple records both sides of the proposal-permission boundary. Its command fields are the action taps of The four-stream action tap tuple taken at intervention time: \(\NominalControlCommand\) is the requested or mapped command (\(\RequestedActionTap\) or \(\MappedCommandTap\), whichever the source emits), \(\CertifiedControlAction\) is the enforced command \(\EnforcedCommandTap\), and the measured response \(\MeasuredResponseTap\) lives in \(\MechanicalPlantState\). The fields are:

  • \(\MicrocontrollerClockTime\) is the monotonic event timestamp recorded in nanoseconds by the safety microcontroller hardware clock, mapped to other subsystem clocks through a qualified clock-conversion method with an explicit error bound. Expressing values in nanosecond units does not imply nanosecond-level inter-device synchronization accuracy.
  • \(\RecordedAuthorityState\) records the active discrete authority state (AUTO, PEND, BLEND, MAN, FALLBACK, or PROTECTIVE).
  • \(\MechanicalPlantState\) captures the instantaneous measured plant state, including joint angles, velocities, motor phase currents, and IMU linear accelerations.
  • \(\NominalControlCommand\) is the proposed plant control setpoint requested by the cognitive policy or human operator console.
  • \(\CertifiedControlAction\) is the control command admitted by the enforcer, including a feasible CBF-filtered proposal or the selected qualified fallback. Associated event metadata must distinguish admission, modification, and rejection; Closed-form quadratic programming and torque projection specifies the projection path.
  • \(\JointTorque\) is the actuator torque command dispatched across the real-time fieldbus to the motor inverters. It is still a command; delivered motor torque can be estimated from the measured phase currents in \(\MechanicalPlantState\) using a qualified motor model and explicit uncertainty bounds.
  • \(\text{ID}_{\text{operator}}\) identifies the authenticated cryptographic entity holding active proposal rights.
  • \(\RemainingExecutionLease\) records the remaining time on the active execution lease (\(\ChunkLease\) or \(\ManualLease\), minus the elapsed duration \(\MicrocontrollerClockTime - \LeaseRefreshInstant\)).

Omitting any of these fields breaks post-incident reconstruction. Without the proposal \(\NominalControlCommand\), a dispatched torque \(\JointTorque\) cannot show whether the policy or operator asked for an erratic action. Without the enforcer output \(\CertifiedControlAction\), the log cannot show whether the safety barrier modified that request. Without \(\RemainingExecutionLease\), it cannot show whether authority had already lapsed into a fallback mode when the command was evaluated. Chained with cryptographic integrity proofs and clock-synchronization metadata, these tuples form the per-tick half of the authority record that section 14.8 specifies.

Three architectural requirements ensure that logged tuples serve as reliable forensic evidence. First, timestamps must date physical events rather than software handling. A software interrupt handler that samples the clock only after being scheduled records its own execution delay rather than the event time. Under variable scheduling jitter and interrupt latency, this delay can easily invert the apparent chronological order of an intervention command and a mechanical contact. Protective inputs and fieldbus ingress packets must therefore be timestamped directly at the hardware boundary through timer edge capture and network controller hardware counters. When clock-conversion error, sensor exposure windows, and sampling intervals overlap between two events, the log must explicitly record their relative order as indistinguishable rather than asserting an unverified sequence.

Second, the storage pipeline must be strictly tiered by guarantee level. Minimal evidence tuples stream to a bounded ring buffer in non-volatile memory on the safety microcontroller. The serialized tuple size multiplied by the control tick rate sets a continuous write bandwidth that hardware qualification tests must prove the device sustains under all operating regimes, with buffer overflows explicitly flagged rather than silently dropped. High-bandwidth perception streams, including camera frames, point clouds, and policy internal activations, flow to a best-effort buffer on the primary host. This high-volume traffic is never permitted to contend for the safety microcontroller’s memory or bus bandwidth.

↩︎ The multi-camera ingestion rate that this best-effort buffer must absorb is established in Ingestion rate and the preservation requirement.

Third, loss must be declared explicitly. When buffer exhaustion or bus stalls occur, the logger must record a missing entry or overwritten frame as a declared gap in the evidence, never as an implied safe pass. A declared gap alerts both post-incident auditors and downstream training pipelines that the trajectory continuity was broken, whereas a silent drop makes two separated ticks appear contiguous in time.

Upon receiving an intervention trigger, such as an operator takeover command, an emergency stop assertion, or an enforcer barrier violation, the logger freezes a configured pre-trigger window (\(\PreTriggerWindowDuration =\) 5 s) and transitions to recording a dedicated post-trigger segment. The pre-trigger duration matches the 4.5 s design out-of-loop takeover latency characterized in section 14.2.2, rounded up to whole seconds. This window preserves the design takeover interval, but policy divergence may begin before the operator notices it, so its duration alone does not establish that the complete causal chain remains in the log. Operational sites that measure longer takeover tails must lengthen \(\PreTriggerWindowDuration\) accordingly. Overwritten frames or dropped samples within this interval remain declared gaps in the evidence. Intervention and Recovery Data uses this same window as its lookback horizon, searching it for the divergence point \(\DivergenceInstant\) and detailing why learner-visited states during drift are essential for offline policy training. The authority record provides the cryptographic ownership stamps that allow the training pipeline to distinguish unprivileged policy proposals from enforcer interventions and operator overrides.

Finally, the operational record must reveal any subsequent tampering or truncation. Each entry carries a keyed cryptographic link over its predecessor,12 while periodic authenticated anchors are written to replay-protected storage located outside the logger’s primary write path. Rewritable non-volatile storage with authenticated writes satisfies this requirement, avoiding the need for dedicated write-once media. Under this design, investigators can determine exactly which log prefix each anchor authenticates, verify that cryptographic keys and anchors remained in trusted custody, and flag any unanchored tail or missing block as a declared gap. Hardware-level tamper resistance and loss declaration protect record integrity and expose missing evidence; they do not establish that a sensor reading is accurate. To serve post-incident reconstruction and downstream policy training, however, these raw evidence streams must be bound into a formal, machine-checkable artifact: the authority record.

12 Linked Hash Chains: Haber and Stornetta (1991) proposed linking each timestamp to the cryptographic hash of its predecessor, ensuring that no historical record can be backdated or inserted without invalidating every subsequent link. The authority record inherits both this tamper-evident property and its limit, since an adversary possessing the signing keys and complete control over the storage device can recompute the entire chain. To protect against retroactive tampering, commitment anchors must be mirrored to an independent, replay-protected storage domain outside the logger’s mutable write path.

Haber, Stuart, and W. Scott Stornetta. 1991. “How to Time-Stamp a Digital Document.” Journal of Cryptology 3 (2): 99–111. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF00196791.

14.8 The Authority Record

When an incident investigator asks, weeks after an abrupt blocked-aisle takeover, which entity drove the mobile base as it neared the rack end, the machine’s recorded telemetry provides evidence bounded by its capture coverage, clock alignment, independent plant measurements, and trusted custody. The authority record bridges real-time supervisory arbitration with downstream verification and release claims. To establish this audit trail, the authority record consumes two upstream contracts. From the enforcement record of The Enforcement Record, it ingests the rungs of the fallback ladder so that every logged fallback explicitly identifies the rung executed by the permission path. From the placement record of The Placement Record, it incorporates the permission path’s declared timing bounds and bus arbitration settings, which every operator command also crosses, together with the placement record’s loaded-test field, which stays empty until verification fills it. The record shows who held each channel at each tick; it does not show who decided the speed, the staffing, or the operating domain that put the machine there.

↪ The record of who decided that the machine could be in that situation at all, with a named owner for each decision, is developed in The Approval Record.

To these upstream contracts, the authority record adds two deliverables of its own. The static authority manifest translates the capability matrix of section 14.2 into a machine-checkable contract. It binds every actuator channel to its authorized actors, operational preconditions, preemption tiers, and transition deadlines, and the safety microcontroller verifies and seals this manifest before the actuator drivers energize. Complementing the manifest, the record’s per-tick log forms an independent stream that records, for every control cycle, which entity held kinetic authority alongside the commands proposed, admitted, and dispatched, paired with independently measured physical response. In this stream, a missing cycle registers as an explicit communication or capture gap rather than an implied pass. The static manifest establishes the legal rules of interaction, while the per-tick log supports checking recorded runtime adherence under the capture, synchronization, custody, and declared-gap assumptions of section 14.7.

14.8.1 The static authority manifest and capability binding

If an operator’s permissions or a channel’s rate limits can be edited in software while the machine runs, a post-incident log cannot prove which rules the arbiter was supposed to enforce at the moment of a takeover. The static authority manifest fixes those rules before the actuators energize: loading and cryptographically sealing the manifest during system initialization binds every subsequent event to the permissions and physical limits in force at boot. It specifies every permitted interaction as a verifiable contract between the physical platform and potential command sources. Each entry delineates a distinct physical authority domain using a hardware actuator mask, an authorized entity class (such as a local physical operator, a remote teleoperation console, an autonomous trajectory policy, or an emergency stop loop), and an integer preemption tier. Before any command vector enters the actuation pipeline, the permission path verifies the sender’s cryptographic role credentials against this manifest. Over the network, an operator’s command arrives as an OPERATOR payload under the same proposal header, carrying the operator’s role, the requested channel mask, the command vector, a monotonically increasing nonce, and a keyed authentication tag.

In addition to credential validation, each manifest entry enforces four operational invariants on its assigned channel:

  1. Single-writer mutual exclusion: Hardware access controls guarantee that exactly one entity writes to an actuator channel at any given instant.
  2. Execution lease duration (\(\ManualLease\)): For human operators, the lease defines the maximum tolerable silence before the enforcer revokes authority and triggers a state-dependent fallback.
  3. Command rate ceiling (\(\CommandRateCeiling\)): A hardware slew limit caps how rapidly a requested command vector may change between successive cycles, preventing abrupt step inputs.
  4. Bumpless blending duration (\(\BlendingTransitionWindow\)): A transition window enforces a \(\Glyph{C}^2\) continuous authority transfer ramp that preserves the kinodynamic feasibility bounds established in Kinodynamic Feasibility, requiring a qualified executable profile and a feasible state-matched fallback at every transition state if communication severs. The fallback depends on surviving sensing, controller, and energy premises; duration and continuity alone do not establish it.

14.8.2 Per-tick evidence tuples and the fault injection suite

A general telemetry tuple that records only a single machine-wide mode cannot show which channels were mid-blend, which fallback rung fired, or why a higher-tier source preempted a lower one. Every entry in the authority record’s per-tick log therefore expands the evidence tuple of section 14.7 with further fields. A field names the specific actuator channels governed by the arbitration decision. Another gives the ladder rung of any active FALLBACK. Others give the root cause of any preemption and the instantaneous blend factor \(\BumplessTransferBlend(\TimeInstant)\) of the enforcer-managed profile. The last is a cryptographic hash link to the preceding log block, authenticated through the platform’s protected anchoring scheme. The versioned static manifest supplies the decoding context, specifying physical units, field serialization order, clock synchronization mappings with their associated error bounds, credential validation rules, and hardware access control masks.

↪ Byte layout: The operator payload’s and the authority record’s fields, types, and offsets are given in Authority record and operator payload.

Verification draws its test inputs from the static manifest and its evidence from the event schema. The manifest’s authority_claims field hands Forward physical analysis and common-cause modes four authority rules by identifier, each of which becomes a hardware-in-the-loop fault case on the machine:

  • A1. Exactly one qualified enforcer output path writes each actuator channel. Source arbitration and hardware access controls prevent a second sender from bypassing that path while the policy and remote assistant both propose commands; successful bus arbitration alone does not satisfy this claim.
  • A2. No lower-tier source bypasses an active higher-tier source or the independent permission check. An autonomous policy proposal during MAN is ignored, and an assistant command that opposes the enforcer’s admitted barrier limit is clamped or rejected.
  • A3. An unacknowledged handover or stalled manual lease decays authority by \(\HandoverTimeout\) of section 14.2.4, initiating controlled fallback by the earlier of the configured lease ceiling and the clearance-derived deadline of equation 4. This clearance deadline has a partial-budget upper bound of 751.6 ms at the 0.75 m/s slow-down before an in-loop takeover, and 100.8 ms for a handover requested by the policy at the 1.3 m/s aisle speed. Both values represent additional-delay allocations in the capture-origin budget. The runtime deadline evaluates the actual event history and future delay budgets without double-charging for elapsed capture-to-admission transit.
  • A4. The enforcer rejects replayed veto_path, manual_drive, or accept_item packets, and packets with invalid authentication tags or expired sequence counters, without modifying still-valid channel authority. It logs each rejection in the evidence tuple while allowing the active lease to age normally.

These authority rules turn the event log into a checkable contract. The same synchronized hardware timestamps also mark which controller generated each segment of an intervention trajectory, and that separation must exist before any segment can serve as a training target for imitation learning.

14.8.3 Causal masking and recovery eligibility

If a training pipeline feeds an entire rescued episode into behavioral cloning without filtering, the policy learns to imitate the autonomous drift that caused the near-miss, the enforcer’s synthetic blend spline, and the operator’s initial startled oscillations. The curation rules of Intervention and Recovery Data prevent that contamination by deciding which logged samples may train a policy: drift commands are quarantined, drift states are relabeled only by an automated expert, and only stabilized, reviewed recovery is admitted. Each rule keys on a timestamp that the authority record supplies, so a logging system that records intervention episodes for downstream policy retraining must preserve controller ownership, target meaning, and declared sample eligibility. This chapter’s conservative direct-imitation policy applies causal intervention masking to that record. Its optimization objective over recorded trajectories is formulated as: \[\MaskedImitationLoss(\ModelParameters) = \mathbb{E}_{(\MechanicalPlantState, \ImitationTargetCommand, \CausalInterventionMask) \sim \MaskedImitationDataset} \left[ \sum_{k=0}^{\BehaviorCloningHorizon} \CausalInterventionMask(\ControlSampleIndex) \cdot \ell\left(\Policy(\MechanicalPlantState_k), \ImitationTargetCommand(\ControlSampleIndex)\right) \right] \tag{8}\] where \(\ell(\Policy(\MechanicalPlantState_k), \ImitationTargetCommand(\ControlSampleIndex))\) denotes the policy prediction loss (such as mean squared error across action chunks or score-matching denoising loss for diffusion policies), \(\CausalInterventionMask(\ControlSampleIndex) \in \{0, 1\}\) is a binary causal mask, and \(\ImitationTargetCommand(\ControlSampleIndex)\) is the supervised training target. Each sample’s mask and target are evaluated at that sample’s logged hardware timestamp. The target carries its own symbol because it is not the admitted command \(\CertifiedControlAction\) of the evidence tuple; in each interval it is chosen from the logged fields, or supplied by an expert, as the interval list below states. Constructing the training dataset \(\MaskedImitationDataset\) requires selective transformation rather than wholesale ingestion of raw telemetry. While every logged state preserves its chronological position in the trajectory, its supervision target \(\ImitationTargetCommand(\TimeInstant)\) and mask \(\CausalInterventionMask(\TimeInstant)\) depend on the physical regime of the recording interval. As a result, the command dispatched to the hardware is not necessarily the training target. Deterministic authority transitions stamped by the safety microcontroller establish controller ownership. Empirical stabilization criteria, task validity checks, and the availability of expert labels, in turn, determine which samples receive \(\CausalInterventionMask(\TimeInstant) = 1\).

One intervention, four intervals; only validated, stabilized recovery trains directly as a human demonstration.

A supervisory intervention does not occur as a single monolithic demonstration. Instead, it spans four distinct physical intervals along the execution timeline:

  1. Compounding policy drift (\(\DivergenceInstant \le \TimeInstant < \CommitmentInstant\)): The autonomous policy \(\Policy\) drifts away from the expert state distribution, accumulating compounding errors while the human supervisor perceives the anomaly, orients, and decides to intervene (the pre-commit latency modeled in equation 1).
    • Masking policy: The autonomous proposals \(\NominalControlCommand(\TimeInstant)\) executed during this drift are quarantined from training targets. Without an external expert label, the system sets \(\CausalInterventionMask(\TimeInstant) = 0\).
    • Relabeling: The learner-visited states \(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant)\) remain in the log for the corrective aggregation of Intervention and Recovery Data, whose relabeling rule accepts only an automated expert’s labels. A drift state therefore enters training with \(\CausalInterventionMask(\TimeInstant) = 1\) and target \(\ImitationTargetCommand(\TimeInstant) = \pi^*(\MechanicalPlantState_t)\) only when such an expert, for example an omniscient trajectory planner with privileged simulation state, supplies the correction.
  2. Atomic handover and enforcer blend (\(\CommitmentInstant \le \TimeInstant < \CommitmentInstant + \BlendingTransitionWindow\)): The safety microcontroller latches the channel endpoints and computes a \(\Glyph{C}^2\) continuous smoothstep trajectory between controllers.
    • Masking policy: The system sets \(\CausalInterventionMask(\TimeInstant) = 0\) unconditionally. Dispatched setpoints \(\ControlCommand(\TimeInstant)\) during this window are synthetic interpolation splines generated by the enforcer’s deterministic quintic polynomial profile. Because they reflect neither learned neural policy intent nor human cognitive demonstration, training on this interval would condition the network to emit open-loop kinematic ramps detached from environmental feedback.
  3. Operator re-orientation and hunting (\(\CommitmentInstant + \BlendingTransitionWindow \le \TimeInstant < \OperatorStabilizationInstant\)): Upon receiving the active manual lease (\(\ManualLease\)), the operator experiences neuromuscular latency, visual parallax, and control re-orientation, producing high-jerk, oscillatory corrections while regaining situational awareness.
    • Masking policy: The system enforces \(\CausalInterventionMask(\TimeInstant) = 0\). Bainbridge documented this control hunting directly. An operator whose active manual skill has lapsed during passive monitoring makes abrupt step corrections, oscillating around the setpoint before settling into smooth tracking (Bainbridge 1983). Control stabilization often takes significantly longer than the physical handover itself. In driving simulator studies, operators resuming control from automated systems required approximately 35–40 s before their lateral steering stabilized (Merat et al. 2014), at least 7× the length of the machine’s 5 s pre-trigger window. Consequently, post-trigger masking cannot rely on a fixed time constant; logging systems must evaluate empirical stabilization criteria. Discarding these initial frames prevents hesitation and oscillatory hunting from corrupting the imitation buffer. This exclusion involves an engineering trade-off, because interval 3 also contains the operator’s initial corrective inputs, which certain human-gated methods retain (table 8). The enforcer marks the stabilization boundary \(\OperatorStabilizationInstant\) only after tracking error and command slew have remained within calibrated limits for a declared dwell interval. Later violations mask the affected samples again. The limits are: \[\lVert \TakeoverTrackingError(\TimeInstant) \rVert \le \StabilizationErrorThreshold \quad \text{and} \quad \lVert \HumanCommandRate(\TimeInstant) \rVert \le \StabilizationSlewCeiling.\] Here, \(\TakeoverTrackingError(\TimeInstant)\) is the difference between the admitted manual setpoint and the measured plant response, \(\HumanCommandRate(\TimeInstant)\) is the time derivative of the operator’s command, and the parameter \(\StabilizationSlewCeiling\) denotes a channel-specific slew ceiling, representing an acceleration derivative (jerk) only when the underlying command vector represents acceleration. Both thresholds must hold continuously across the dwell window before the logger lifts the mask.
  4. Stabilized expert recovery (\(\TimeInstant \ge \OperatorStabilizationInstant\)): The operator executes coherent, task-directed recovery maneuvers once measured control metrics stabilize. Smooth motion alone does not validate a recovery; the episode must also satisfy formal task-completion criteria before its samples qualify as expert targets.
    • Masking policy: The system sets \(\CausalInterventionMask(\TimeInstant) = 1\) with imitation target \(\ImitationTargetCommand(\TimeInstant) = \HumanControlCommand(\TimeInstant)\) exclusively for stabilized segments that pass task-validity review. All other telemetry frames remain masked from direct imitation, so this interval is the only post-takeover segment the curation rules admit as expert recovery data.
Merat, Natasha, A. Hamish Jamson, Frank C. H. Lai, Michael Daly, and Oliver M. J. Carsten. 2014. “Transition to Manual: Driver Behaviour When Resuming Control from a Highly Automated Vehicle.” Transportation Research Part F: Traffic Psychology and Behaviour 27: 274–82. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.trf.2014.09.005.

Feeding unsegmented telemetry directly into behavioral cloning or diffusion policies injects synthetic enforcer ramps and operator control transients into the training distribution. The causal mask \(\CausalInterventionMask(\TimeInstant)\) and target vector \(\ImitationTargetCommand(\TimeInstant)\) use the commit and state timestamps recorded by the safety microcontroller to enforce controller ownership boundaries, bounded by hardware clock synchronization and sampling limits.

The binary causal mask represents the most conservative member of a broader algorithmic spectrum. Contemporary human-in-the-loop learning methods treat interventions in fundamentally different ways: as direct state labels, as importance weights over surrounding trajectories, or as negative reinforcement signals indicating policy failure. Table 8 maps five representative paradigms onto these four operational intervals. For instance, Human-Gated DAgger (HG-DAgger) grants the human supervisor full authority upon takeover and aggregates only the human’s executed commands, leaving interval 1 unmasked but unlabeled (Kelly et al. 2019). Sirius generalizes \(\CausalInterventionMask(\TimeInstant)\) by assigning categorical importance weights across sample classes; it assigns zero weight to a fixed pre-intervention window while upweighting human recovery trajectories (Liu et al. 2023). Specifically, Sirius sizes its pre-intervention window to 15 robot actions (approximately 2 s), derived from observed human reaction times. While Sirius fixes this window a priori, the authority record timestamps the commit and supports reconstruction of the drift interval once \(\DivergenceInstant\) is identified from the retained evidence, a duration that the out-of-loop takeover budget of section 14.2.2 shows can be 2.2× longer. Meanwhile, Reinforcement Learning from Intervention Feedback (RLIF) eliminates the assumption of human optimality altogether, treating the intervention event as a negative reward penalty within an off-policy reinforcement learning framework (Luo et al. 2024). Similarly, Human-in-the-Loop Sample-Efficient Robot Learning (HIL-SERL) incorporates human interventions as targeted correction buffers for real-world reinforcement learning (Luo et al. 2025). Finally, RECAP, which trains the \(\pi^{*}_{0.6}\) vision-language-action model, blends autonomous rollouts, teleoperated interventions, and demonstrations using advantage conditioning, explicitly labeling human takeover corrections as positive-advantage actions (Physical Intelligence et al. 2025).

Individual learning algorithms change, but every one of them must answer the same three systems questions: who held write authority over each sample, whether the takeover serves as a supervised label, an importance weight, or a reward penalty, and which physical intervals enter the training set. Downstream training pipelines cannot recover those ownership boundaries from trajectory kinematics alone. Recording the commit, blend-end, and stabilization timestamps on the safety microcontroller’s clock grounds dataset curation and fleet-level intervention rates directly in hardware measurements.

When an engineering team assembles the safety case for release (Matching the argument to the machine), the authority record also supplies the primary evidence that runtime handovers obey rules A1 through A4 (claim C6 in Claim-argument-evidence review for the warehouse mobile manipulator). That release dossier pairs the static authority manifest with the loaded stopping and blending measurements from section 14.4 and hardware-in-the-loop traces that verify an unbroken audit chain. Because a handover that looks clean on the communication bus can still slip a wheel on a wet floor or jolt a loaded tote rack, the dossier always checks bus timestamps against independent plant sensors. It treats measured preemption times as empirical percentiles rather than analytical worst-case bounds.

↩︎ The distinction between an empirical latency percentile and a response-time bound is established in Real-Time Command Transport and Bus Determinism.

Checkpoint 14.3: Authority evidence and causal masks

The authority record is useful twice, once to an investigator and once to a learner, and both uses depend on the same ownership timestamps.

14.9 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Flipping a bit in an authority register does not complete a safe supervisory handover. A real-time physical AI system must preserve its dynamic stopping margin and protective enforcement functions throughout every stage of transfer. Furthermore, downstream imitation learning pipelines cannot treat intervention logs as unblemished expert recoveries without explicit causal filtering. When systems fail in deployment, the breakdown almost always traces back to one of four subtle architectural traps.

One 500 ms window overruns at aisle speed and idles at a crawl.

Pitfall: Treating human takeover timeout as a static time delay rather than a dynamic spatial stopping budget.

Consider a mobile manipulator that initiates a takeover request at its operational 1.3 m/s aisle speed under a statically configured 500 ms acknowledgment window. If the controller waits out that full window before commanding brakes, the vehicle covers 0.65 m of unbraked travel. When combined with the jerk-limited resident stop profile and actuator execution overheads, the total stopping distance ends 0.52 m beyond the 1.10 m rack-end clearance, causing a physical collision. Conversely, at an out-of-loop crawl speed of 0.15 m/s, that same window consumes negligible distance, leaving 5.8 s of margin before fallback braking must begin. The wait costs travel in proportion to speed, and the stop that follows grows with its square, so a timeout that leaves ample response time at a crawl overruns the clear distance at aisle speed. Real-time supervisory handovers cannot treat human response intervals as fixed wall-clock durations. The permissible wait time must be computed dynamically from instantaneous velocity, remaining obstacle clearance, and validated brake deceleration envelopes. Autonomous fallback must begin the moment the remaining clearance matches the required stopping profile.

A supervisory timeout is not a static timer; it is a dynamically bounded spatial margin that must fit the instantaneous stopping budget.

Architect’s countermeasure: On every control cycle, evaluate the instantaneous obstacle clearance against a complete stopping budget composed solely of future sensing delays, brake actuation lag, and jerk-limited deceleration. Dynamically recompute a candidate absolute deadline, anchored to a consistent time origin, from equation 4, which gives the longest wait whose delay travel and stop suffix still fit inside the remaining clearance. If a newly calculated deadline is earlier than the currently active deadline, tighten the expiration threshold immediately; never extend or restart an active countdown based on optimistic recalculations. Account for sensor pipeline latency consistently, and do not subtract historical capture-to-enforcement delay from clearance measurements that have already physically reflected that travel. If supervisory acknowledgment does not arrive before the deadline expires, immediately trigger the validated autonomous fallback brake sequence.

Fallacy: Human manual takeover should bypass automated safety barriers and grant unconstrained, open-ended actuator authority.

When a remote operator takes manual control of a mobile manipulator to steer it around an unexpected obstacle, the chosen steering commands might vector the chassis directly toward a warehouse rack upright. Granting manual control alters only the origin of proposed motion setpoints; it cannot alter chassis momentum, tire-floor friction coefficients, or environmental obstacle boundaries. The low-level safety enforcer must therefore evaluate human manual proposals against the formal admissible motion set on every cycle. If the operator commands a trajectory that risks collision or dynamic instability, the enforcer projects the command onto the nearest safe, kinodynamically feasible trajectory. If no safe trajectory exists within the admissible envelope, the enforcer rejects the proposal entirely and triggers an immediate autonomous fallback. Manual intervention grants the operator authority to guide high-level intent, but mechanical stresses, obstacle clearance, and brake margins remain hard physical constraints enforced at the joint actuator boundary.

Furthermore, manual intervention must operate under strictly bounded, perishable leases rather than open-ended authority grants. Transient wireless packet loss or operator distraction can easily leave stale velocity vectors active in flight. To prevent uncommanded runaway motion, every intervention session requires a cryptographically renewed manual lease calibrated to the active operational mode’s stopping reserve. In this warehouse aisle scenario, the system enforces a 100 ms manual lease. If periodic, authenticated heartbeat renewals fail to arrive before the lease expires, the safety microcontroller immediately revokes proposal authority and executes an autonomous fallback stop independently of host software or operator intervention.

Manual intervention transfers the authority to propose setpoints, but never the authority to bypass physical barrier certificates or operate on stale communication.

Architect’s countermeasure: Channel all teleoperation and manual override inputs through the enforcer’s control barrier certificates and kinodynamic clamping filters. When an operator command exceeds dynamic rollover thresholds, threatens structural shear limits, or breaches clearance buffers, the enforcer automatically clamps the actuation setpoint to the boundary of the safe set or initiates an emergency stop. Subordinate all manual authority to a hardware-timed watchdog lease sized to the active mode’s stopping reserve, guaranteeing that lease expiration automatically executes a deterministic brake or hold sequence without software intervention.

Pitfall: Relying on cryptographic authentication without rate limiting the traffic that reaches the verifier.

Cryptographic authentication guarantees message authenticity and integrity, but it introduces an asymmetric computation cost that an adversary or a misbehaving transceiver can exploit to starve real-time processing. Suppose an admission controller validates incoming signed remote takeover requests (section 14.5) using an embedded microcontroller that requires 1.8 ms to verify each Ed25519 digital signature. Malformed inputs may be rejected cheaply, but well-formed invalid signatures can still reach the expensive verification path. The following queue model charges every such request the stated verification time.

If a malfunctioning or compromised device on the network link floods the interface with bogus signed manual_drive packets, as few as 55 forged requests queued ahead of a critical veto_path message from a remote supervisor will delay its verification to 100.80 ms. At aisle speed, this delay exceeds the fallback deadline \(\FallbackDeadline\) of 100.78 ms. When charging the allocated downstream admission delay \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\), the usable admission margin shrinks to 37.5 ms, meaning just 20 forged packets suffice to cause a deadline violation. Sustaining this denial-of-service attack requires an injection rate of only 546 packets per second, a trivial load for even a low-bandwidth industrial wireless channel.

Substituting symmetric MACs shifts the computational bottleneck to the network transceiver without eliminating the underlying vulnerability. Validating a MAC requires far fewer cycles, so roughly 20,306 forged frames within the same window are needed to induce a miss, yet the queue remains susceptible to line-rate saturating traffic. The robot’s independently wired protective stops and hardware lease timers must never share queues with network ingress. Those local safety channels must remain fully operational even when the cryptographic verifier is saturated. To protect real-time responsiveness, the ingestion pipeline must deploy hardware mailbox filters and per-source rate limiters ahead of the verification queue, and safety qualification tests must evaluate worst-case intervention latency under full adversarial packet inundation.

Cryptographic authentication protects command integrity but introduces an asymmetric availability risk, because an admission verifier spends finite compute cycles rejecting forged traffic before it can service critical safety messages.

Architect’s countermeasure: Deploy hardware mailbox filtering and per-source token-bucket rate limiters13 on the fieldbus interface ahead of the software verification queue. In addition, route emergency protective stops and fail-safe interlocks through dedicated, hardwired electrical circuits that bypass digital networking and cryptographic parsing entirely.

13 Token Bucket: A limiter holds up to \(\TokenBucketDepth\) tokens, refills at rate \(\TokenBucketRefillRate\), and admits a packet only by spending a token, so a source may burst \(\TokenBucketDepth\) packets but never sustain more than \(\TokenBucketRefillRate\). Placed before the verifier, it bounds the forged signatures that can arrive within \(\FallbackDeadline\) at \(\TokenBucketDepth + \TokenBucketRefillRate\,\FallbackDeadline\). For the single-source queue modeled here, keeping that bound below the 55 forgeries that push a genuine veto past its deadline, or 20 once \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) is charged, sizes \(\TokenBucketDepth\) and \(\TokenBucketRefillRate\) from the stopping budget rather than from link capacity.
