A deployed ingress must also bound aggregate traffic across sources, include already queued work and legitimate traffic, and prevent forged source identifiers from bypassing those limits.

Fallacy: Training directly on raw human intervention logs captures expert demonstrations.

Training on raw intervention logs commits the learner-action contamination of Intervention and Recovery Data. The raw telemetry of a rescued episode is not a continuous expert demonstration. It stitches the policy’s diverging trajectory to the operator’s reaction latency, the corrective jerks as the operator regains situational awareness, and the overshoots before stabilization, and a policy cloned from it reproduces the oscillations that made the takeover necessary.

The methods in table 8 require attribution of who generated each state-action pair and a declared interpretation of the sample as a target, a weight, or a reward. Neither a coarse session-level takeover timestamp nor an active manual authority bit guarantees that recorded telemetry is fit for policy training. This chapter’s conservative direct-imitation policy segments intervention trajectories into distinct causal regimes and masks transitional dynamics; the alternative objectives in table 8 retain some early corrections.

Unsegmented intervention telemetry records human cognitive lag and abrupt corrective overshoots; naively cloning these trajectories trains policies to reproduce the instability that triggered the takeover.

Architect’s countermeasure: For this chapter’s conservative direct-imitation corpus, apply causal intervention masking (equation 8), taking the interval boundaries from the commit, blend-end, and stabilization timestamps on the safety microcontroller’s monotonic hardware clock. Only Interval 4, validated post-stabilization guidance, trains as a human demonstration (\(\CausalInterventionMask(\TimeInstant)=1\)); Interval 1 drift states train only under an automated expert’s labels, and everything else stays in the record for forensics.

14.10 Summary

Supervisory intervention transfers the right to propose, never the right to admit. A capability matrix grants each authenticated role named operations on named actuator channels, each with a preemption tier and a clearance-derived deadline, while the enforcer checks every command from every source against the same physical envelope. Enforcement changes a proposal and leaves the proposer in place; intervention changes the proposer and leaves the check in place, and the machine needs both.

A takeover is a delay inserted before braking on a moving machine, so it is priced in the stopping budget rather than in the user interface. At the 1.3 m/s aisle speed the subtotal before dispatch-to-admission travel leaves 131.0 mm, a waiting window of 100.8 ms, and only 37.5 ms once the 63.3 ms dispatch-to-admission allocation of The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval is charged. Sourced human responses of 605 ms in the loop and 4.5 s out of it cap the request speed at 0.797 m/s and 0.189 m/s once it is charged. No brake, however strong, admits the aisle speed, because the waiting term rather than the braking term spends the clearance. The design therefore slows to 0.75 m/s before an anticipated in-loop request, crawls at 0.15 m/s or stops first before an out-of-loop one, and answers a newly discovered hazard with its own local stop. None of these speeds qualifies until loaded measurements confirm the allocation and the braking premises.

Authority is bounded and moved by mechanisms that never wait on the network. Execution leases on the safety microcontroller’s clock, the autonomous chunk lease and the 100 ms manual lease, revoke a silent proposer and select the fallback that matches the machine’s state. A four-phase handshake commits ownership on one control tick for an explicit channel mask, the enforcer remains the only writer to the actuators, and every timer that expires leads to a defined state. Authentication keeps forged commands out of admission, but only a rate limiter ahead of the verifier keeps forged traffic from delaying a genuine veto, and hardwired protective inputs bypass both.

Changing the proposer does not change the physics of the command. A fixed-endpoint \(\Glyph{C}^2\) smoothstep bounds the command rate between latched endpoints, but it spends clearance. The base window is 528 ms at every speed because the jerk bound belongs to the loaded base, and at the 0.75 m/s request speed no braking blend fits the clearance that remains. Within the stated one-dimensional stopping model and boundary conditions, no smoothed braking under the same bounds beats the resident profile, so the base receives the resident stop while the arm blends over 38 ms.

The authority record turns every handover into evidence. It binds the sealed static manifest to per-tick evidence tuples that record who held each channel and what was proposed, admitted, and dispatched alongside independent measurements of the response, chains them against tampering, and declares every gap. It draws its fallback rungs from the enforcement record and its declared timing bounds from the placement record, whose loaded measurements are still to come. The same ownership timestamps serve two readers. An investigator can reconstruct an incident from them, provided independent current, encoder, and contact measurements confirm what the commands did, and a learner can apply this chapter’s conservative four-interval direct-imitation mask, under which only validated, stabilized human recovery supplies post-takeover demonstration targets. Other learning objectives use the same provenance with different eligibility rules (table 8). Table 9 summarizes the governing quantitative relations for supervisory intervention, detailing the specific operational or physical quantity bounded by each equation.

Key Takeaways: Supervisory intervention and authority bounds
  • State-dependent fallback and monotonic leases: Bind each proposer to an explicit, mode-specific lease and stopping reserve. Lease expiry invokes the validated local fallback response appropriate for the current physical state, along with the sensing, drive energy, braking, and holding premises required to execute it safely. For manual teleoperation, the reference design chooses a 100 ms lease, bounded from below by heartbeat loss and jitter and from above by the clearance its extra travel over the chunk lease consumes after the takeover.
  • Clearance governs waiting: A takeover delay is travel before braking, so its allowance comes from the stopping budget, not from a configured timer. The subtotal before dispatch-to-admission travel allows 100.8 ms of waiting at the aisle speed, 37.5 ms once the 63.3 ms admission allocation is charged, and 751.6 ms at the 0.75 m/s slow-down, which still leaves 83.3 ms after an in-loop takeover and the charge. Price human responses at design percentiles, not averages. On the complete budget, the 605 ms in-loop design response caps the request speed at 0.797 m/s and the 4.5 s out-of-loop response caps it at 0.189 m/s. Dispatch the fallback at the earliest of the protocol, lease, evidence, and clearance deadlines, and stop before handing an unexpected hazard to an out-of-loop operator.
  • Single-writer actuator ownership: Explicit source arbitration and exclusive enforcer command ownership eliminate conflicting actuator writes; low-level bus arbitration alone cannot establish safety authority. Every request, commit, and confirmation message must explicitly declare its channel mask. A partial transfer, such as reassigning arm control while the base keeps navigating autonomously, is admissible only while the console annunciates the untransferred channel’s owner; otherwise that channel holds. A four-phase protocol (Request, Acknowledge, Commit, Confirm) transitions authority deterministically. The commit transition revokes the autonomous policy token on the designated channels and latches its last admitted command. No proposer holds the proposal token during BLEND, while the enforcer owns the command; the arbiter grants the token to the operator only after the profile completes, and the enforcer then checks human proposals against the same physical envelopes as the policy’s.
  • Smooth command blending and haptic passivity: A fixed-endpoint \(\Glyph{C}^2\) acceleration profile bounds peak jerk during authority transitions, and its additional stopping displacement must be reserved in the braking envelope. If the system incorporates bilateral force reflection, the console feedback loop requires its own passivity and stability argument evaluated under realistic network latency, packet loss, and actuator dynamics.
  • Independent stopping paths: Dedicated, hardwired protective circuits execute risk-assessed mechanical and electrical stops independent of software. Network-delivered commands undergo cryptographic admission checks: corrupted or unauthenticated packets are dropped at ingress, whereas a failure to refresh a valid lease triggers an autonomous local fallback.
  • Causal data attribution: Training on intervention logs requires preserving controller provenance and declaring target and eligibility rules. This chapter’s conservative direct-imitation mask \(\CausalInterventionMask(\TimeInstant)\) (equation 8) preserves learner-visited drift states for failure analysis and automated relabeling, zeros out synthetic enforcer blending splines (\(\BlendingTransitionWindow\)) and initial operator hunting, and admits only validated, stabilized human recovery as post-takeover demonstration targets. Other objectives may retain or weight early corrections differently (table 8).
  • Authority as auditable evidence: The authority record logs per-tick evidence tuples \(\langle \MicrocontrollerClockTime, \RecordedAuthorityState, \MechanicalPlantState, \NominalControlCommand, \CertifiedControlAction, \JointTorque, \text{ID}_{\text{operator}}, \RemainingExecutionLease\rangle\) into tamper-evident cryptographic hash chains, treating a missing entry as an explicit fault rather than an implied pass. The manifest’s four authority rules serve as primary test targets for verification fault injection; their passing logs, anchored by the authority record, are the evidence the release argument needs before it may admit remote takeover.

A person’s authority over an embodied physical AI system is granted on verifiable evidence and expires when that evidence lapses, as the fourth law, permission must be earned (principle 4), requires of every privilege the machine honors. Fresh credentials, checked plant state, a feasible target and fallback reserve, and a successfully committed handshake support the grant of authority; the manual lease bounds how long the grant remains valid while its physical premises continue to hold. When operator inputs cease renewing the lease, authority immediately lapses to a verified local fallback governed by the hardware permission path. Preemption tiers do not persist across a lease expiration: a remote assistant who previously commanded the mobile base retains no residual authority once the lease expires. Re-establishing control under any proposer requires satisfying the reset conditions of the authority state machine and presenting fresh, validated credentials.

14.11 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

Supervisory control emerged as an engineering discipline when teleoperated systems outgrew direct manual control. In deep-sea exploration and hazardous environment manipulation, transmission latency and high operator workloads made continuous teleoperation fragile. Sheridan and Verplank (1978) formalized the division of labor between human operators and machine intelligence, grading autonomy into explicit levels that range from complete manual execution to autonomous computer execution that ignores human input. Sheridan (1992) consolidated these insights into a unified systems theory of human supervisory control, modeling how human operators alternate between planning, programming, monitoring, intervening, and learning from automated subroutines. Expanding on this taxonomy, Parasuraman et al. (2000) decoupled automation across four functional dimensions: sensory information acquisition, information analysis, decision selection, and action execution, assigning distinct operational levels to each stage. Meanwhile, industrial process automation approached supervisory handovers from a control-theoretic perspective. Switching a closed-loop plant between manual operation and automatic tracking without inducing mechanical shock or actuator saturation requires the idle controller to track the active plant state continuously. This bumpless transfer technique, detailed as standard practice by Åström and Hägglund (2006), provides the classical foundation that this chapter’s enforcer generalizes: smoothly blending between verified endpoint states while strictly respecting physical actuator limits.

Åström, Karl Johan, and Tore Hägglund. 2006. Advanced PID Control. ISA-The Instrumentation, Systems,; Automation Society.
Brooks, Rodney. 1986. “A Robust Layered Control System for a Mobile Robot.” IEEE Journal on Robotics and Automation 2 (1): 14–23.

Human factors engineering revealed why relying on a passive human operator as an emergency fallback is fundamentally flawed. In a landmark critique, Bainbridge (1983) articulated the central irony of automation: automating routine operations leaves the human supervisor responsible solely for rare, anomalous edge cases, precisely when sustained inactivity has degraded both situational awareness and manual control proficiency. Sarter and Woods (1995) examined cockpit automation to trace the root causes of mode confusion, demonstrating how silent automation state changes lead pilots to issue inputs that are entirely correct for their assumed operational mode but hazardous for the aircraft’s actual mode. Endsley (1995) formalized the cognitive basis of operator intervention, decomposing situation awareness into three hierarchical tiers: perceiving sensory cues, comprehending the operational state, and projecting system trajectories into the near future. Physical latency imposes strict mathematical limits on these interactions. Ferrell (1965) demonstrated that when loop delays exceed a fraction of a second, human operators inevitably degrade into a disjointed “move-and-wait” strategy to avoid destabilizing the physical plant. In bilateral teleoperation with force reflection, delayed feedback injects non-physical energy into the loop and can destabilize it. Anderson and Spong (1989) addressed this instability through a passive communication-channel construction within a specified bilateral control architecture. Closed-loop stability also depends on the channel terminations and the coupled controllers and plant. Niemeyer and Slotine (1991) reformulated the communication-channel passivity condition using wave variables. Variable delay, packet loss, sampling, and saturation require the additional analysis identified in Passivity and Energy-Bounded Interaction. At the architectural level, arbitrating between competing subsystem commands using rigid priorities directly descends from the layered suppression and inhibition mechanisms pioneered by Brooks (1986) in the subsumption architecture.

Machine learning turned supervisory intervention into a source of training data. Ross et al. (2011) proved that naive behavioral cloning fails under distributional drift because the autonomous policy inevitably encounters states absent from the expert demonstrations; robust training requires querying the expert along the learner’s own induced state distribution via the DAgger algorithm. To reduce human fatigue, Kelly et al. (2019) introduced Human-Gated DAgger, delegating continuous control to the autonomous policy and recording demonstrations only when the human supervisor actively intervenes. Modern embodied systems generalize these intervention boundaries into core training signals. Sirius (Liu et al. 2023) uses intervention transitions as prioritized sample weights in offline policy optimization. Luo et al. (2024) converts supervisory takeovers into reinforcement learning reward penalties. Luo et al. (2025) uses intervention corrections to drive real-world robotic reinforcement learning safely on physical hardware. Every data-driven approach relies on unambiguous execution provenance: the system must record exactly which agent (human supervisor, machine policy, or safety enforcer) held authority over every actuator command. Tamper-evident logging draws on a deep cryptographic lineage, from the cryptographically linked timestamps of Haber and Stornetta (1991) to the hierarchical authentication structures of Merkle (1988). This chapter integrates these disparate systems threads into a single runtime abstraction: a per-tick authority record that aligns the handover sequence with the physical stopping budget and retains ownership boundaries for downstream policy learning. The arbiter and enforcer establish exclusive channel ownership; per-channel announcements support mode awareness, and the record supports evaluating both under its timing and capture assumptions.

Kelly, Michael, Chelsea Sidrane, Katherine Driggs-Campbell, and Mykel J. Kochenderfer. 2019. “HG-DAgger: Interactive Imitation Learning with Human Experts.” IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 8077–83. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA.2019.8793698.
Liu, Huihan, Soroush Nasiriany, Lance Zhang, Zhiyao Bao, and Yuke Zhu. 2023. “Robot Learning on the Job: Human-in-the-Loop Autonomy and Learning During Deployment.” Robotics: Science and Systems (RSS). https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2023.XIX.005.
Luo, Jianlan, Perry Dong, Yuexiang Zhai, Yi Ma, and Sergey Levine. 2024. “RLIF: Interactive Imitation Learning as Reinforcement Learning.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Luo, Jianlan, Charles Xu, Jeffrey Wu, and Sergey Levine. 2025. “Precise and Dexterous Robotic Manipulation via Human-in-the-Loop Reinforcement Learning.” Science Robotics 10 (105): eads5033. https://doi.org/10.1126/scirobotics.ads5033.
Merkle, Ralph C. 1988. “A Digital Signature Based on a Conventional Encryption Function.” Advances in Cryptology — CRYPTO ’87, Lecture notes in computer science, vol. 293: 369–78. https://doi.org/10.1007/3-540-48184-2_32.

Six works trace this lineage from the ironies of automation to learning from interventions:

  • Bainbridge (1983) articulates why automating routine operations deprives human operators of the running mental model and manual dexterity required to handle anomalous edge cases, establishing the engineering motivation for executing a safe dynamic stop before requesting intervention.
  • Sheridan (1992) provides the definitive monograph uniting supervisory control, teleoperation architectures, and the dynamics of delayed human-in-the-loop systems.
  • Sarter and Woods (1995) identifies the cognitive mechanics of mode awareness breakdowns in complex cockpits, illustrating the failure modes that per-channel masks and state announcements mitigate in section 14.6.
  • Parasuraman et al. (2000) establishes the multi-dimensional taxonomy of automation across acquisition, analysis, decision, and action, providing the conceptual architecture behind channel-by-channel capability matrices.
  • Anderson and Spong (1989) develops a passive communication-channel construction for delayed bilateral teleoperation, forming the analytical basis for section 14.4.5. Its closed-loop stability argument depends on the specified channel terminations and control architecture.
  • Ross et al. (2011) introduces the DAgger algorithm and demonstrates that imitation learners must be trained against corrections collected on their own visited state distributions, explaining why the authority record must capture policy drift intervals and takeover boundaries.
Bainbridge, Lisanne. 1983. “Ironies of Automation.” Automatica 19 (6): 775–79. https://doi.org/10.1016/0005-1098(83)90046-8.
Sheridan, Thomas B. 1992. Telerobotics, Automation, and Human Supervisory Control. MIT Press.
Sarter, Nadine B., and David D. Woods. 1995. “How in the World Did We Ever Get into That Mode? Mode Error and Awareness in Supervisory Control.” Human Factors 37 (1): 5–19. https://doi.org/10.1518/001872095779049516.
Parasuraman, Raja, Thomas B. Sheridan, and Christopher D. Wickens. 2000. “A Model for Types and Levels of Human Interaction with Automation.” IEEE Transactions on Systems, Man, and Cybernetics - Part A: Systems and Humans 30 (3): 286–97. https://doi.org/10.1109/3468.844354.
Ross, Stéphane, Geoffrey Gordon, and J. Andrew Bagnell. 2011. “A Reduction of Imitation Learning and Structured Prediction to No-Regret Online Learning.” Proceedings of the Fourteenth International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS) 15: 627–35.

What’s Next: From authority revocation to verification
Operational logs alone cannot establish that the arbiter enforces its rules under adverse conditions. Weeks of clean handovers in the authority record rarely contain a duplicate writer, a replayed packet, or a dropped heartbeat, and a record cannot testify about a fault that never occurred. The same gap runs through every record this book has developed, because the chunk lease, the permission path, and the held-up permission rail all describe protective responses that normal operation never exercises. Adversarial Verification creates those faults on purpose. It pairs four physical faults injected as the loaded base approaches the rack end, beginning with a stalled proposer, with the four authority rules A1 through A4 that this chapter’s manifest hands over. It scores each trial against an oracle fixed before the test, measured by instruments the fault cannot reach rather than by the arbiter’s own account of what it did.
